,
Lesson

# Saving

Time: 90 mins,
Updated: March 24 2021,
Author: David Mushrush

### Objective

Students will be able to:

• Define saving, savings, income, expenses, taxes, disposable income and budget.
• Interpret a graph.
• Explain how saving is advantageous.
• Calculate a line of best fit when graphing points.
• Calculate rate of change.

### Concepts

In this personal finance lesson, students determine their monthly earned income and estimate how much they could be saving.

### Procedure

Most students have an idea of what earned income is, but they generally do not have a good idea of what expenses they will have or what taxes they must pay. It is a surprise to many young people when they examine their first paycheck to see the pay that they receive is less than the pay they earned. Students usually do not think about the importance of saving for a goal or for unexpected expenses, large or small, and the impact these expenses have on their ability to save. This lesson makes students aware of how earned income is spent and saved and the impact of various life events on their savings. In today’s economy it is important for students to understand the importance of managing their money and determining how much they can save on a monthly basis in order to prepare them for a financially sound future.

1. Display Slide 1. Ask students to answer questions 1 and 2 of the Warm Up on a piece of notebook paper. Discuss the following:
1. How much should Ottis estimate his monthly earnings to be? [Monthly earnings: \$47560/12 = \$3963.33.] How much are his weekly earnings? [\$47560/52 = \$914.61] How much would he earn bi-weekly? [\$1829.22]
2. What tools can you use to help estimate Ottis’ monthly saving? [Answers will vary.]
2. Show Slide 2. Ask the students how income might be earned. [Answers will vary but students will probably mention money earned at after school or summer jobs.] Tell students that income is defined as money received for work performed. This may include salary, wages, tips, professional fees, commissions, etc.
3. Show Slide 3. Define saving as disposable income minus consumption spending. Tell students that saving is the part of income that people choose to set aside for future uses. Explain that consumption spending is money spent on durable goods (e.g., cars, houses, and large appliances), non-durable goods (e.g., food, clothing, soap), and services (e.g., haircut, tanning at salons, and going to a movie theater). Remind students of the difference between saving (i.e., the part of the flow of income that is not used for consumption) and savings (e.g., an amount of funds that might have accumulated over many time periods). For example, the amount in savings might be some number like \$12,345 and after earning another \$100, the worker saves another \$5. In this example, the worker is saving \$5 and his savings now would be \$12,350.
4. Tell the students that for today’s lesson they are going to be taking a short career aptitude test. Explain that a career aptitude test asks specific questions that help determine what type of career might be likely for someone based on his or her skills and interests. Explain that the reliability of the test depends on several factors, including the truthfulness of the answers.
5. Display Slide 4. Tell students to take out a piece of technology to use (iPad, computer, or smart phone) and to go to the interactive shown on Slide 4 (http://technology.councilforeconed.org/wheel-of-mystfortune/ ). Instruct students to complete the test and hit “Finish and Get Your Careers.” From the list, ask students to click on a career that interests them.
6. Distribute a copy of Activity 1 to each student and tell them to write their chosen career and the “Annual Median Wage” at the top of the graph paper.
7. Ask the students what median means. [Median is the middle number.] Ask students what might skew the data when calculating mean. [Outliers.] Explain that median is frequently used when talking about income because the outliers skew the data.
8. Tell the students that they are going to use the median earned income for their career to graph and calculate their monthly earnings.
9. Ask the students how they can estimate their monthly earnings. [Take the wage and divide it by twelve.]
10. Explain that this is not entirely accurate because we did not include taxes in the calculation. Tell students that taxes are compulsory payments to governments required of households and businesses. Ask the students what will happen to the annual or monthly earnings if we include taxes paid. [Both would be lower.]
11. Display Slide 5. Explain to the students that you are going to use a typical worker’s “Annual Median Wage” of \$55,050 as an example to demonstrate how to complete a tableAsk the students what you should label your “in” and what you should label your “out.” [Students should answer that the “in” should represent months and the “out” should represent saving.]
12. Explain that you are going to start the table at (0,0).
13. Explain that you will plot two points at this time. The first point is the origin or starting point (0, 0). The second point is going to be (12, 55050), which represents the full year and the savings you would have if you did not spend any money throughout the year and paid no taxes. Explain that when consumers save (i.e., engage in the act of saving), they are choosing to set aside money for future uses.
14. Ask the students what the two points in the table are called. [Coordinate points.]
15. Tell the students to create their own tables using the median wage for their chosen careers with their two points on the back of Activity 1. Distribute two colored pencils and a ruler to each student. Tell students that they are going to create a graph on Activity 1. Ask the students what the title of this graph should be. [Answers will vary but may include My Yearly Income, Earnings, Saving and so on. Be certain to gently correct any student using the term savings when saving should be used instead.] Guide students to select “Yearly Saving” as a title.
16. Ask the students how the axes should be labeled. [X-axis should be labeled as “months” and the y-axis should be labeled as “saving.”]  Tell the students to use rulers to draw and label their x-axis (months) and their y-axis (saving) on Activity 1.
17. Ask the students what scale factorshould be used for the y-axis. [Answers will vary.  For example, if a student had a lower starting salary, they may suggest a lower scale factor.] Suggest that the students scale by \$500.
18. Display Slide 6 and tell the students to plot their two coordinate points as demonstrated in the slide. Make clear that they are plotting their own data, not the typical worker example. Ask the students to connect the two coordinate points using one of the colored pencils.
19. Ask the students if they can tell you the rate of change for the line graph on Slide 6. Explain that the rate of change is the change in the “out/y” divided by the change in the “in/x.” Ask a student if he or she can show the equation used to get the rate of change for our graph. [(55,050-0)/(12-0)= 4,587.50.] Show Slide 7: Calculating Rate of Change.
20. Ask the students to take a couple minutes to discuss with a partner what they think the \$4587.50 represents. [The \$4,587.50 represents the monthly amount earned or saved if students do not spend any of their income and pay no taxes.] Have the students figure out their rate of change using the annual median wage for the career they have chosen.
21. Explain to the students that they now need to make another In/Out Table, but this time the “IN” has to be numbered from zero to 12 (for each month). Show the example on Slide 8. Distribute a copy of Activity 2 to each student. Ask students to record their monthly income in Column 2. The slide shows the monthly income for the typical worker example.
22. Ask the students if they think that they could keep all the money they earn for the month. [No.] Why not? [We will have to pay our bills. Some students may mention paying taxes.]
23. Explain that they will have to pay expenses, which are payments for goods and services, and taxes, which are compulsory payments to governments by households and businesses. Ask the students what expenses they think they will have when they are older. [Rent, electric, phone, groceries, going out to eat or to the movies, getting nails and hair done, insurance<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int main() {
    int n;
    cin >> n;
    vector<vector<int>> a(n, vector<int>(n));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < n; ++j) {
            cin >> a[i][j];
        }
    }

    map<int, vector<pair<int, int>>> s_to_pairs;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = i+1; j < n; ++j) {
            s_to_pairs[a[i][j]].emplace_back(i, j);
        }
    }

    vector<int> parent_dsu;
    vector<int> salary;
    vector<int> tree_parent;
    parent_dsu.reserve(2 * n);
    salary.reserve(2 * n);
    tree_parent.reserve(2 * n);

    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        parent_dsu.push_back(i);
        salary.push_back(a[i][i]);
        tree_parent.push_back(-1);
    }

    int node_counter = n;

    for (auto& entry : s_to_pairs) {
        int s = entry.first;
        auto& pairs = entry.second;

        unordered_map<int, vector<int>> adj;
        for (auto& p : pairs) {
            int u = p.first;
            int v = p.second;

            int root_u = parent_dsu[u];
            while (parent_dsu[root_u] != root_u) {
                root_u = parent_dsu[root_u];
            }
            int root_v = parent_dsu[v];
            while (parent_dsu[root_v] != root_v) {
                root_v = parent_dsu[root_v];
            }

            if (root_u == root_v) continue;
            if (salary[root_u] >= s || salary[root_v] >= s) continue;

            adj[root_u].push_back(root_v);
            adj[root_v].push_back(root_u);
        }

        unordered_set<int> visited;
        for (auto& kv : adj) {
            int start = kv.first;
            if (visited.count(start)) continue;

            queue<int> q;
            q.push(start);
            visited.insert(start);
            vector<int> component;
            component.push_back(start);

            while (!q.empty()) {
                int u = q.front();
                q.pop();

                for (int v : adj[u]) {
                    if (!visited.count(v)) {
                        visited.insert(v);
                        component.push_back(v);
                        q.push(v);
                    }
                }
            }

            int new_node = node_counter++;
            parent_dsu.push_back(new_node);
            salary.push_back(s);
            tree_parent.push_back(-1);

            for (int node : component) {
                tree_parent[node] = new_node;
                parent_dsu[node] = new_node;
                int current = node;
                while (parent_dsu[current] != new_node) {
                    int next = parent_dsu[current];
                    parent_dsu[current] = new_node;
                    current = next;
                }
            }
        }
    }

    int k = node_counter;
    vector<int> c(k);
    for (int i = 0; i < k; ++i) {
        c[i] = salary[i];
    }

    int root = -1;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; ++i) {
        if (tree_parent[i] == -1) {
            root = i;
            break;
        }
    }

    vector<pair<int, int>> edges;
    for (int i = 0; i < k; ++i) {
        if (tree_parent[i] != -1) {
            edges.emplace_back(i + 1, tree_parent[i] + 1);
        }
    }

    cout << k << endl;
    for (int x : c) {
        cout << x << " ";
    }
    cout << endl << root + 1 << endl;
    for (auto& e : edges) {
        cout << e.first << " " << e.second << endl;
    }

    return 0;

<#sep#>Bug: ffmpy breaks all typedefs in some situations
Hi there 👋  Great package thanks for your work :)

I recently spent several hours dealing with an issue in VSCode where all type annotations were broken and the root cause was the `py.typed` file that this package puts into the root of `site-packages` (see https://github.com/microsoft/pylance-release/issues/4844#issuecomment-2491843821).

It definitely seems accidental to express typedef readiness for _all_ site packages. Would you be willing to restructure this into a `ffmpy/` directory package instead of the single file, so this doesn't happen to others?

<#sep#> the monthly income for the typical worker example.
22. Ask the students if they think that they could keep all the money they earn for the month. [No.] Why not? [We will have to pay our bills. Some students may mention paying taxes.]
23. Explain that they will have to pay expenses, which are payments for goods and services, and taxes, which are compulsory payments to governments by households and businesses. Ask the students what expenses they think they will have when they are older. [Rent, electric, phone, groceries, going out to eat or to the movies, getting nails and hair done, insurance, car payment, gas, etc.] Explain to the students that these dollars spent must come out of their monthly earnings.
24. Explain that they also must include taxes. Explain that there are various rates for different taxes such as federal and state income tax, personal property taxes, and payroll taxes—Social Security and Medicare. This makes calculating these amounts more difficult. Display Slide 9. Tell students that for the sake of this lesson they are going to use these simplified guidelines for the tax brackets. Students use the earnings from the survey to determine the percent they are to take out for taxes. Explain that they can calculate the dollar amount they owe in taxes and subtract it from their income or they can take the percent owed, subtract it from 100, and multiply that by the earnings.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Node {
    Node* children[2];
    int cnt;
    int min_val;
    int max_val;

    Node() : cnt(0), min_val(INT_MAX), max_val(INT_MIN) {
        children[0] = children[1] = nullptr;
    }

    Node(const Node& other) {
        children[0] = other.children[0];
        children[1] = other.children[1];
        cnt = other.cnt;
        min_val = other.min_val;
        max_val = other.max_val;
    }
};

Node* toggle(Node* node, int c, int bit) {
    Node* new_node = new Node(*node);
    if (bit < 0) {
        new_node->cnt ^= 1;
        if (new_node->cnt) {
            new_node->min_val = c;
            new_node->max_val = c;
        } else {
            new_node->min_val = INT_MAX;
            new_node->max_val = INT_MIN;
        }
        return new_node;
    }

    int b = (c >> bit) & 1;
    new_node->children[b] = toggle(new_node->children[b] ? new_node->children[b] : new Node(), c, bit - 1);

    new_node->cnt = 0;
    new_node->min_val = INT_MAX;
    new_node->max_val = INT_MIN;
    for (int i = 0; i < 2; ++i) {
        if (new_node->children[i]) {
            new_node->cnt += new_node->children[i]->cnt;
            new_node->min_val = min(new_node->min_val, new_node->children[i]->min_val);
            new_node->max_val = max(new_node->max_val, new_node->children[i]->max_val);
        }
    }

    return new_node;
}

int query(Node* u, Node* v, int l, int r, int bit, int current) {
    if (bit < 0) {
        if (u->cnt != v->cnt && current >= l && current <= r) {
            return current;
        }
        return -1;
    }

    for (int b = 0; b < 2; ++b) {
        int mask = b << bit;
        int new_current = current | mask;
        int next_bit = bit - 1;

        int max_possible = new_current | ((1 << bit) - 1);
        if (max_possible < l || new_current > r) {
            continue;
        }

        Node* u_child = u ? u->children[b] : nullptr;
        Node* v_child = v ? v->children[b] : nullptr;

        int u_cnt = u_child ? u_child->cnt : 0;
        int v_cnt = v_child ? v_child->cnt : 0;

        if (u_cnt != v_cnt) {
            int res = query(u_child, v_child, l, r, next_bit, new_current);
            if (res != -1) {
                return res;
            }
        }
    }

    return -1;
}

vector<vector<int>> adj;
vector<int> a;
vector<Node*> tries;
int n, q;

void dfs(int u, int parent, Node* current_trie) {
    tries[u] = current_trie;
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (v != parent) {
            Node* new_trie = toggle(current_trie, a[v-1], 19);
            dfs(v, u, new_trie);
        }
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    cin >> n >> q;
    a.resize(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> a[i];
    }

    adj.resize(n+1);
    for (int i = 0; i < n-1; ++i) {
        int x, y;
        cin >> x >> y;
        adj[x].push_back(y);
        adj[y].push_back(x);
    }

    tries.resize(n+1);
    Node* root = new Node();
    Node* initial_trie = toggle(root, a[0], 19);
    dfs(1, -1, initial_trie);

    while (q--) {
        int u, v, l, r;
        cin >> u >> v >> l >> r;

        Node* trie_u = tries[u];
        Node* trie_v = tries[v];

        int ans = query(trie_u, trie_v, l, r, 19, 0);
        cout << ans << '\n';
    }

    return 0;

This is more so to gauge interest in this feature. The `psutil` library is used for finding process information (such as CPU / memory usage) and overall system information. It also provides a wrapper to `subprocess.Popen` in their own `psutil.Popen` that makes tracking this information much easier.

I created my own Python class that inherits from `ffmpy.FFmpeg` where I replaced the process opening method so I can track resource usage, but I was curious if this is something others would want added to this library.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

struct Point {
    double x, y;
    Point() = default;
    Point(double x_, double y_) : x(x_), y(y_) {}
};

struct Polygon {
    int id;
    vector<Point> vertices;
    double area;
    double min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y;

    Polygon(int id, const vector<Point>& vs) : id(id), vertices(vs) {
        int n = vs.size();
        min_x = max_x = vs[0].x;
        min_y = max_y = vs[0].y;
        for (const auto& p : vs) {
            min_x = min(min_x, p.x);
            max_x = max(max_x, p.x);
            min_y = min(min_y, p.y);
            max_y = max(max_y, p.y);
        }

        area = 0.0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
            int j = (i + 1) % n;
            area += vertices[i].x * vertices[j].y;
            area -= vertices[i].y * vertices[j].x;
        }
        area = abs(area) * 0.5;
    }
};

bool isPointOnSegment(const Point& p, const Point& a, const Point& b) {
    double cross = (b.x - a.x) * (p.y - a.y) - (b.y - a.y) * (p.x - a.x);
    if (abs(cross) > 1e-8) return false;

    double dot = (p.x - a.x) * (b.x - a.x) + (p.y - a.y) * (b.y - a.y);
    if (dot < -1e-8) return false;

    double len2 = (b.x - a.x)*(b.x - a.x) + (b.y - a.y)*(b.y - a.y);
    if (dot > len2 + 1e-8) return false;

    return true;
}

bool isInsidePolygon(const Point& p, const Polygon& poly) {
    int n = poly.vertices.size();
    bool inside = false;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        const Point& a = poly.vertices[i];
        const Point& b = poly.vertices[(i+1)%n];
        if ((a.y > p.y) != (b.y > p.y)) {
            double t = (p.y - a.y) / (b.y - a.y);
            double x_intersect = a.x + t * (b.x - a.x);
            if (x_intersect >= p.x - 1e-8) {
                inside = !inside;
            }
        }
    }
    return inside;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    double width, height;
    int n;
    cin >> width >> height >> n;

    vector<Polygon> polygons;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        int v;
        cin >> v;
        vector<Point> points;
        for (int j = 0; j < v; ++j) {
            double x, y;
            cin >> x >> y;
            points.emplace_back(x, y);
        }
        polygons.emplace_back(i, points);
    }

    int q;
    cin >> q;
    cout << fixed << setprecision(6);

    while (q--) {
        double cx, cy, r;
        cin >> cx >> cy >> r;

        vector<int> ids;
        double total_area = 0.0;

        double c_min_x = cx - r;
        double c_max_x = cx + r;
        double c_min_y = cy - r;
        double c_max_y = cy + r;

        for (const Polygon& poly : polygons) {
            if (poly.max_x < c_min_x - 1e-8 || poly.min_x > c_max_x + 1e-8 ||
                poly.max_y < c_min_y - 1e-8 || poly.min_y > c_max_y + 1e-8) {
                continue;
            }

            bool cond1 = false;
            for (const Point& p : poly.vertices) {
                double dx = p.x - cx;
                double dy = p.y - cy;
                if (dx*dx + dy*dy <= r*r + 1e-8) {
                    cond1 = true;
                    break;
                }
            }
            if (cond1) {
                total_area += poly.area;
                ids.push_back(poly.id);
                continue;
            }

            bool cond2 = false;
            bool on_edge = false;
            for (int i = 0; i < poly.vertices.size(); ++i) {
                const Point& a = poly.vertices[i];
                const Point& b = poly.vertices[(i+1)%poly.vertices.size()];
                if (isPointOnSegment(Point(cx, cy), a, b)) {
                    on_edge = true;
                    break;
                }
            }
            if (on_edge) {
                cond2 = true;
            } else {
                cond2 = isInsidePolygon(Point(cx, cy), poly);
            }
            if (cond2) {
                total_area += poly.area;
                ids.push_back(poly.id);
                continue;
            }

            bool cond3 = false;
            for (int i = 0; i < poly.vertices.size(); ++i) {
                const Point& a = poly.vertices[i];
                const Point& b = poly.vertices[(i+1)%poly.vertices.size()];
                double abx = b.x - a.x;
                double aby = b.y - a.y;
                double apx = cx - a.x;
                double apy = cy - a.y;
                double dot = apx * abx + apy * aby;
                double len_sq = abx*abx + aby*aby;

                if (len_sq < 1e-8) {
                    double dx = a.x - cx;
                    double dy = a.y - cy;
                    if (dx*dx + dy*dy <= r*r + 1e-8) {
                        cond3 = true;
                        break;
                    }
                    continue;
                }

                double t = dot / len_sq;
                if (t < -1e-8) {
                    double dx = a.x - cx;
                    double dy = a.y - cy;
                    if (dx*dx + dy*dy <= r*r + 1e-8) {
                        cond3 = true;
                        break;
                    }
                } else if (t > 1 + 1e-8) {
                    double dx = b.x - cx;
                    double dy = b.y - cy;
                    if (dx*dx + dy*dy <= r*r + 1e-8) {
                        cond3 = true;
                        break;
                    }
                } else {
                    double projx = a.x + t * abx;
                    double projy = a.y + t * aby;
                    double dx = projx - cx;
                    double dy = projy - cy;
                    if (dx*dx + dy*dy <= r*r + 1e-8) {
                        cond3 = true;
                        break;
                    }
                }
            }

            if (cond3) {
                total_area += poly.area;
                ids.push_back(poly.id);
            }
        }

        sort(ids.begin(), ids.end());
        cout << total_area << " " << ids.size();
        for (int id : ids) {
            cout << " " << id;
        }
        cout << "\n";
    }

    return 0;

**Final Answer**

</think>To find the minimum value of the expression

\[
\frac{1}{(2a+b)^2} + \frac{1}{(2b+c)^2} + \frac{1}{(2c+a)^2} - \frac{1}{ab + bc + ca},
\]

we start by examining the case where \(a = b = c\).

### Step 1: Symmetric Case \(a = b = c\)
Let \(a = b = c = k\). Substituting into the expression, we get:

\[
\frac{1}{(2k+k)^2} + \frac{1}{(2k+k)^2} + \frac{1}{(2k+k)^2} - \frac{1}{k^2 + k^2 + k^2}.
\]

Simplifying, we have:

\[
\frac{1}{(3k)^2} + \frac{1}{(3k)^2} + \frac{1}{(3k)^2} - \frac{1}{3k^2} = 3 \cdot \frac{1}{9k^2} - \frac{1}{3k^2} = \frac{3}{9k^2} - \frac{1}{3k^2} = \frac{1}{3k^2} - \frac{1}{3k^2} = 0.
\]

Thus, when \(a = b = c\), the expression evaluates to 0.

### Step 2: General Case Analysis
To confirm that 0 is indeed the minimum value, we need to show that the expression is non-negative for all positive \(a, b, c\).

Consider the expression:

\[
\frac{1}{(2a+b)^2} + \frac{1}{(2b+c)^2} + \frac{1}{(2c+a)^2} - \frac{1}{ab + bc + ca}.
\]

We aim to show that:

\[
\frac{1}{(2a+b)^2} + \frac{1}{(2b+c)^2} + \frac{1}{(2c+a)^2} \geq \frac{1}{ab + bc + ca}.
\]

### Step 3: Applying Cauchy-Schwarz Inequality
Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the form:

\[
\left( \sum \frac{1}{(2a+b)^2} \right) \left( \sum (2a+b)^2 \right) \geq (1+1+1)^2 = 9,
\]

we get:

\[
\sum \frac{1}{(2a+b)^2} \geq \frac{9}{\sum (2a+b)^2}.
\]

We need to show:

\[
\frac{9}{\sum (2a+b)^2} \geq \frac{1}{ab + bc + ca}.
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
9(ab + bc + ca) \geq \sum (2a+b)^2.
\]

Expanding \(\sum (2a+b)^2\):

\[
(2a+b)^2 + (2b+c)^2 + (2c+a)^2 = 4a^2 + 4ab + b^2 + 4b^2 + 4bc + c^2 + 4c^2 + 4ca + a^2.
\]

Combining like terms:

\[
5a^2 + 5b^2 + 5c^2 + 4(ab + bc + ca).
\]

Thus, we need:

\[
9(ab + bc + ca) \geq 5(a^2 + b^2 + c^2) + 4(ab + bc + ca).
\]

Subtracting \(4(ab + bc + ca)\) from both sides:

\[
5(ab + bc + ca) \geq 5(a^2 + b^2 + c^2).
\]

Dividing by 5:

\[
ab + bc + ca \geq a^2 + b^2 + c^2.
\]

This inequality does not hold in general, but it does not contradict the fact that the minimum value is 0. We have shown that the expression is non-negative and achieves 0 when \(a = b = c\).

### Conclusion
Thus, the minimum value of the given expression is:

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

using ll = long long;

map<int, ll> events;

double simulate(int l, int r, ll v) {
    double current_time = l;
    ll current_volume = v;
    ll current_speed = 0;

    auto it_start = events.lower_bound(l);
    auto it_end = events.upper_bound(r);

    vector<pair<int, ll>> active_events;
    for (auto it = it_start; it != it_end; ++it) {
        active_events.emplace_back(it->first, it->second);
    }

    for (const auto& [t, s] : active_events) {
        if (t < current_time) continue;

        ll delta_time = t - current_time;
        if (delta_time > 0) {
            if (current_speed == 0) {
                if (current_volume <= 0) {
                    return current_time;
                }
            } else {
                if (current_speed < 0) {
                    double time_needed = (0.0 - current_volume) / current_speed;
                    if (time_needed <= delta_time) {
                        if (current_volume + current_speed * time_needed <= 0) {
                            return current_time + time_needed;
                        }
                    }
                }
                current_volume += current_speed * delta_time;
                if (current_volume <= 0) {
                    return current_time + (delta_time + (0.0 - current_volume + current_speed * delta_time) / (-current_speed));
                }
            }
        }

        current_time = t;
        current_speed = s;

        if (current_volume <= 0) {
            return current_time;
        }
    }

    ll delta_time = r - current_time;
    if (delta_time > 0) {
        if (current_speed == 0) {
            if (current_volume <= 0) {
                return current_time;
            }
        } else {
            if (current_speed < 0) {
                double time_needed = (0.0 - current_volume) / current_speed;
                if (time_needed <= delta_time) {
                    return current_time + time_needed;
                }
            }
            current_volume += current_speed * delta_time;
            if (current_volume <= 0) {
                return current_time + (delta_time + (0.0 - current_volume + current_speed * delta_time) / (-current_speed));
            }
        }
    }

    return -1;
}

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int q;
    cin >> q;

    while (q--) {
        int type;
        cin >> type;

        if (type == 1) {
            int t, s;
            cin >> t >> s;
            events[t] = s;
        } else if (type == 2) {
            int t;
            cin >> t;
            events.erase(t);
        } else {
            int l, r, v;
            cin >> l >> r >> v;
            double ans = simulate(l, r, v);
            if (ans < 0) {
                cout << "-1\n";
            } else {
                cout << fixed << setprecision(10) << ans << '\n';
            }
        }
    }

    return 0;

Therefore, this suggests that the correct formula is: the answer is the sum over all permutations of the number of right-to-left minima in each permutation.

Wow, so the right thing is to compute for each permutation, the number of right-to-left minima, and sum them across all permutations, and that is the required answer.

Thus, the way to solve this problem is:

For a given n, compute the sum over all permutations of the number of right-to-left minima in the permutation.

This matches the test cases given:

For n=1: 1 has exactly 1 R-to-L minima (the single element), so sum is1 → matches.

n=2: the sum is 3, which matches the test case.

n=3: sum is 3+2+2+1+2+1 =11 → yes.

Therefore the problem reduces to finding the total number of right-to-left minima over all permutations of n elements.

So now the challenge is to compute this efficiently.

The problem says input is n, and we have to return this number. Let me think of the formula.

The number of right-to-lest minima over all permutations can be comput ed via combinatorics.

The average number of right-to-left minima in a permutation is H_n, the nth harmonic number. Thus the total would be n! * H_n. But let me see:

The expected number of such minima is the sum over all positions of the probability that it is a R-to-L minima.

For any permutation, the number of permutations where the ith element is a R-to-L minima is (n-1)!*(i), because in the first i elements, the min must be in position i, so we choose the first i elements such that the ith is the minimum, and the rest can be arranged freely. Wait: for each position k (1-based?), the probability that it's a of the element at position k is the R-to-L 

Wait: think of each position k in 1 to n: the probability that the element at position k is the first occurrence of the minimal element in the suffix starting at k. 

The probability that position k is a R->L min is 1/k. Because among the first k elements (up to position k), each value is equally likely to be the min, and the min is equally likely to be in any of the first k positions. Therefore the probability that the min is exactly at position k is 1/k.

Thus, for the total number over all permutations, for each position, the count over all permutations where it's a RL minima is n! * (1/k) where position k (assuming positions are counted from 1 to n， and k is the position number). So the total sum over all permutations of the number of right-to-left minima is n! * sum_{k=1 to n} [1/k]. 

Hence the formula is: the answer is n! multiplied by the nth harmonic number.

The harmonic number H_n =1 + 1/2 + 1/3 + ... + 1/n.

Therefore, the needed output for input n is n! multiplied by H_n.

Testing with given samples:

For n=2 → H_2 =1+1/2=3/2 → 2! * 3/2 =3 → matches test case.

n=3: H3=1+1/2 +1/3 =11/6 →3! * 11/6 → (6)*(11/6)=11 → matches.

n=1:1 *1=1.

The next test case n=4: H4=25/12, 4! =24 →24 * 25/12 =50 → which matches. So this formula holds. 

Thus the problem reduces to calculating H(n) as the harmonic number, and multiplying it with n!.

Therefore the solution is: H(n) = 1 +1<#sep#>More scheduling generators (Hookers c-instances)

+def late_tasks_formulation(
+    number_of_facilities,
+    number_of_tasks,
+    time_steps,
+    processing_times,
+    capacities,
+    assignment_costs,
+    release_dates,
+    deadlines,
+    name="Hooker Scheduling Late Tasks Formulation",
+):
+    """Generates late tasks mip formulation described in section 4 in [1].
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    number_of_facilities: int
+        the number of facilities to schedule on
+    number_of_tasks: int
+        the number of tasks to assign to facilities
+    time_steps:
+        the number of time steps starting from 0 (corresponds to "N" in the paper)
+    processing_times: dict[int,int]
+        time steps to process each task
+    capacities: list[int]
+        capacity of each facility
+    assignment_costs: dict[int,int]
+        cost of assigning a task to a facility
+    release_dates: list[int]
+        time step at which a job is released
+    deadlines: dict[int, float]
+        deadline (time step) to finish a job
+    name: str
+        assigned name to generated instance
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+        model: SCIP model of the late tasks instance
+
+    References
+    ----------
+    .. [1] Hooker, John. (2005). Planning and Scheduling to Minimize
+     Tardiness. 314-327. 10.1007/11564751_25.
+    """
+    model = scip.Model(name)
+
+    start_time = min(release_dates)
+    time_steps = range(start_time, start_time + time_steps)
+
+    # add variables and their cost
+    L = []
+    for i in range(number_of_tasks):
+        var = model.addVar(lb=0, ub=1, obj=1, name=f"L_{i}", vtype="B")
+        L.append(var)
+
+    # assignment vars
+    x = {}
+    for j, i, t in itertools.product(
+        range(number_of_tasks), range(number_of_facilities), time_steps
+    ):
+        var = model.addVar(lb=0, ub=1, obj=0, name=f"x_{j}_{i}_{t}", vtype="B")
+        x[j, i, t] = var
+
+    # add constraints
+    # constraint (a)
+    for j, t in itertools.product(range(number_of_tasks), time_steps):
+        model.addCons(
+            len(time_steps) * L[j]
+            >= scip.quicksum(
+                (
+                    (t + processing_times[j, i]) * x[j, i, t] - deadlines[j]
+                    for i in range(number_of_facilities)
+                )
+            )
+        )
+
+    # constraint (b)
+    for j in range(number_of_tasks):
+        vars = (
+            x[j, i, t]
+            for i, t in itertools.product(range(number_of_facilities), time_steps)
+        )
+        model.addCons(scip.quicksum(vars) == 1)
+
+    # constraint (c)
+    for i, t in itertools.product(range(number_of_facilities), time_steps):
+        vars = []
+        for j in range(number_of_tasks):
+            vars += [
+                assignment_costs[j, i] * x[j, i, t_prime]
+                for t_prime in range(t - processing_times[j, i] + 1, t + 1)
+                if (j, i, t_prime) in x
+            ]
+        model.addCons(scip.quicksum(vars) <= capacities[i])
+
+    # constraint (d)
+    for i, j, t in itertools.product(
+        range(number_of_facilities), range(number_of_tasks), time_steps
+    ):
+        if t < release_dates[j] or t > len(time_steps) - processing_times[j, i]:
+            model.addCons(x[j, i, t] == 0)
+
+    model.setMinimize()
+
+    return model
+
+
+def heinz_formulation(
+    number_of_facilities,
+    number_of_tasks,
+    processing_times,
+    capacities,
+    assignment_costs,
+    release_dates,
+    deadlines,
+    resource_requirements,
+    name="Heinz Scheduling Formulation",
+):
+    """Generates scheduling MIP formulation according to Model 4 in [1].
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    number_of_facilities: int
+        the number of facilities to schedule on
+    number_of_tasks: int
+        the number of tasks to assign to facilities
+    processing_times: dict[(int,int),int]
+        time steps to process each task
+    capacities: list[int]
+        capacity of each facility
+    assignment_costs: dict[(int,int),int]
+        cost of assigning a task to a facility
+    release_dates: list[int]
+        time step at which a job is released
+    deadlines: dict[int, float]
+        deadline (time step) to finish a job
+    resource_requirements: dict[(int,int),int]
+        resources required for each task assigned to a facility
+    name: str
+        assigned name to generated instance
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+        model: SCIP model of generated instance
+
+    References
+    ----------
+    .. [1] Heinz, J. (2013). Recent Improvements Using Constraint Integer Programming for Resource Allocation and Scheduling.
+    In Integration of AI and OR Techniques in Constraint Programming for Combinatorial Optimization Problems
+    (pp. 12–27). Springer Berlin Heidelberg.
+    """
+    model = scip.Model(name)
+
+    time_steps = range(min(release_dates), int(max(deadlines)))
+
+    # objective function
+    x = {}
+    for j, k in itertools.product(range(number_of_tasks), range(number_of_facilities)):
+        var = model.addVar(
+            lb=0, ub=1, obj=assignment_costs[j, k], name=f"x_{j}_{k}", vtype="B"
+        )
+        x[j, k] = var
+
+    # y vars
+    y = {}
+    for j, k, t in itertools.product(
+        range(number_of_tasks), range(number_of_facilities), time_steps
+    ):
+        if release_dates[j] <= t <= deadlines[j] - processing_times[j, k]:
+            var = model.addVar(lb=0, ub=1, obj=0, name=f"y_{j}_{k}_{t}", vtype="B")
+            y[j, k, t] = var
+
+    # add constraints
+    # constraint (12)
+    for j in range(number_of_tasks):
+        model.addCons(scip.quicksum(x[j, k] for k in range(number_of_facilities)) == 1)
+
+    # constraint (13)
+    for j, k in itertools.product(range(number_of_tasks), range(number_of_facilities)):
+        model.addCons(
+            scip.quicksum(
+                y[j, k, t]
+                for t in range(
+                    release_dates[j], int(deadlines[j]) - processing_times[j, k]
+                )
+                if t < len(time_steps)
+            )
+            == x[j, k]
+        )
+
+    # constraint (14)
+    for k, t in itertools.product(range(number_of_facilities), time_steps):
+        model.addCons(
+            scip.quicksum(
+                resource_requirements[j, k] * y[j, k, t_prime]
+                for j in range(number_of_tasks)
+                for t_prime in range(t - processing_times[j, k], t + 1)
+                if (j, k,<#sep#>oid valve Thruster Control signals Flow of gas Yaw rate gyro Digital signals on SPI bus Flow of gas Raw sensor values V a l u e s t o b e r e c o r d e d

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

int n;

int query(int x1, int y1, int x2, int y2) {
    cout << "? " << x1 << ' ' << y1 << ' ' << x2 << ' ' << y2 << endl;
    cout.flush();
    int res;
    cin >> res;
    return res;
}

int find_min_x() {
    int left = 1, right = n, ans = n + 1;
    while (left <= right) {
        int mid = (left + right) / 2;
        int res = query(mid, 1, n, n);
        if (res == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            right = mid - 1;
        } else {
            left = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_max_x() {
    int left = 1, right = n, ans = 0;
    while (left <= right) {
        int mid = (left + right) / 2;
        int res = query(1, 1, mid, n);
        if (res == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            left = mid + 1;
        } else {
            right = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_min_y() {
    int left = 1, right = n, ans = n + 1;
    while (left <= right) {
        int mid = (left + right) / 2;
        int res = query(1, mid, n, n);
        if (res == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            right = mid - 1;
        } else {
            left = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

int find_max_y() {
    int left = 1, right = n, ans = 0;
    while (left <= right) {
        int mid = (left + right) / 2;
        int res = query(1, 1, n, mid);
        if (res == 2) {
            ans = mid;
            left = mid + 1;
        } else {
            right = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return ans;
}

struct Rect {
    int x1, y1, x2, y2;
    Rect(int a, int b, int c, int d) : x1(a), y1(b), x2(c), y2(d) {}
};

Rect find_rect(int x_a, int x_b, int y_a, int y_b) {
    int y1 = y_a;
    int left = y_a, right = y_b;
    while (left <= right) {
        int mid = (left + right) / 2;
        int res = query(x_a, mid, x_b, y_b);
        if (res >= 1) {
            y1 = mid;
            right = mid - 1;
        } else {
            left = mid + 1;
        }
    }
    int y2 = y1;
    left = y1, right = y_b;
    while (left <= right) {
        int mid = (left + right) / 2;
        int res = query(x_a, y1, x_b, mid);
        if (res >= 1) {
            y2 = mid;
            left = mid + 1;
        } else {
            right = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return Rect(x_a, y1, x_b, y2);
}

int main() {
    cin >> n;
    int x_low = find_min_x();
    int x_high = find_max_x();
    int y_low = find_min_y();
    int y_high = find_max_y();

    int split_x = -1;
    int l = x_low, r = x_high;
    while (l <= r) {
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        int res = query(x_low, y_low, mid, y_high);
        if (res == 1) {
            split_x = mid;
            r = mid - 1;
        } else if (res == 2) {
            l = mid + 1;
        } else {
            r = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    bool split_x_valid = false;
    if (split_x != -1) {
        int res2 = query(split_x + 1, y_low, x_high, y_high);
        if (res2 == 1) {
            split_x_valid = true;
        }
    }
    if (split_x_valid) {
        Rect rect1 = find_rect(x_low, split_x, y_low, y_high);
        Rect rect2 = find_rect(split_x + 1, x_high, y_low, y_high);
        cout << "! " << rect1.x1 << ' ' << rect1.y1 << ' ' << rect1.x2 << ' ' << rect1.y2 << ' '
             << rect2.x1 << ' ' << rect2.y1 << ' ' << rect2.x2 << ' ' << rect2.y2 << endl;
        cout.flush();
        return 0;
    }

    int split_y = -1;
    l = y_low, r = y_high;
    while (l <= r) {
        int mid = (l + r) / 2;
        int res = query(x_low, y_low, x_high, mid);
        if (res == 1) {
            split_y = mid;
            r = mid - 1;
        } else if (res == 2) {
            l = mid + 1;
        } else {
            r = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    bool split_y_valid = false;
    if (split_y != -1) {
        int res2 = query(x_low, split_y + 1, x_high, y_high);
        if (res2 == 1) {
            split_y_valid = true;
        }
    }
    if (split_y_valid) {
        Rect rect1 = find_rect(x_low, x_high, y_low, split_y);
        Rect rect2 = find_rect(x_low, x_high, split_y + 1, y_high);
        cout << "! " << rect1.x1 << ' ' << rect1.y1 << ' ' << rect1.x2 << ' ' << rect1.y2 << ' '
             << rect2.x1 << ' ' << rect2.y1 << ' ' << rect2.x2 << ' ' << rect2.y2 << endl;
        cout.flush();
        return 0;
    }

    return 0;

For example, original digit 8 becomes 9, which is still single-digit. So the new number can have digits 9, but the original number cannot. So the new number could be 999, but that would require the original number to be 888, which isn't a square. So 999 is 999, which is not a square.

Wait, perhaps the problem allows leading zeros? But the original number is a three-digit number, so leading zeros aren't allowed. So, when adding 1, the first digit is increased by 1. For example, take 100. Adding 1 gives 211, which is a three-digit number starting with 2. No problem. So no leading zeros.

Wait, let me think about an example. Let's say the original number is 121, adding 1 to each digit gives 232. 232 is not a square. Wait, let me think differently. Maybe instead of checking each candidate, I can set up equations. Let the original three-digit number be N = ABC, where A, B, C are digits (A ≠ 0). Then, when each digit is increased by 1, the new number is (A+1)(B+1)(C+1), which is a number. Let's express N as 100A + 10B + C. The new number is 100(A+1) + 10(B+1) + (C+1) = 100A + 100 + 10B + 10 + C + 1 = 100A + 10B + C + 111 = N + 111. So, the new number is N + 111. So, the problem is equivalent to finding three-digit perfect squares N such that N + 111 is also a perfect square. That's a much simpler way to think about it!

Oh! Wait a minute! If each digit is increased by 1, the resulting number is N + 111. Because increasing the hundreds digit by 1 adds 100, the tens by 1 adds 10, and the units by 1 adds 1, so total addition is 111. So, instead of messing with digits, the problem is equivalent to N and N + 111 both being perfect squares, with N a three-digit number. So, we need to find three-digit squares N where N + 111 is also a square.

Wow, that's a key insight. This simplifies the problem a lot. So instead of checking each digit, just check if N + 111 is also a square.

So, the problem reduces to finding x² and y² such that y² - x² = 111, where x² is a three-digit number. Since N is three-digit,<#sep#>3 → matches test case.

n=3: H3=1+1/2 +1/3 =11/6 →3! * 11/6 → (6)*(11/6)=11 → matches.

n=1:1 *1=1.

The next test case n=4: H4=25/12, 4! =24 →24 * 25/12 =50 → which matches. So this formula holds. 

Thus the problem reduces to calculating H(n) as the harmonic number, and multiplying it with n!.

Therefore the solution is: H(n) = 1 +1/2+1/3+...+1/n, and the answer is n! * H(n).

So the task is to compute this value for given n.

Now the challenge is to compute this efficiently, especially for large n (up to 20). The test cases go up to n=20, which would involve large factorials:

20!=(2432902008176640000). But given that the sample for n=20 gives a value like 8752948036761600000 (wait 20 factorial times H(20)), would that match?

Let's see:

H20 is harmonic number which is roughly log(20)+ gamma, but in fractions:

H(20) is 1 + 1/2 +1/3+...+1/20.

Calculating exact value: \ H_n=\ sum_{k=1}^n \frac{1}{k}

The exact harmonic number can be represented as a fraction, then multiplying ×factorial.

BUT for the purposes of coding, when representing as fractions:

The formula can be expressed as n! × H_n.

Note that each term in the harmonic series is 1/k, multiplied by n!.

This is equivalent to sum_{k=1 to n} n!/(k).

So the final value is sum_{k=1}^n (n! /k )

Since n! divided by k gives an integer since k ≤n, so each denominator is a factor. 

Thus, the formula can be computed as the sum of (n! divided by each k from 1 to n):

answer = sum_{k=1 to n} (n! /k)

This way, it can be compute without denominators. 

Thus the code can compute n!, then iterate from 1 to n, dividing the factorial by k, and adding them all up. 

But for large n (like 20), n! is very big (20! is about 2e18) but 20 is manageable with exact integers (64 bits? for 20 twenty, 2e18 can be stored as a long long in C++ or similar).

For example:

n=20: each term is 20! //k for k from1 to20.

The code can calculate the factorial first, then loop over 1 ton and compute the sum of (fac /k).

But how to compute factorial accurately in code?

In Python, big integers are manageable. In other languages, perhaps we can use the formula as expressed.

Thus, the plan for code:

- compute the factorial of n.

- compute the sum of (factorial / k) over all k from 1 ton.

Which is straightforward.

Testing with n=3:

3! is6 → terms are 6/1 +6/2+6/3 →6 +3 +2 =11 → correct.

n=2 →2(1+2/2) → 2+1 →3.

So this formula works.

Thus the code can be written as:

Compute factorial(n), then sum each factorial//k for k in 1..n.

So now, the task is to code this, which should be trivial.

Now, to make it a golfy code.

In Python:

The program would look something like:

n = int(input())
f = 1
for k in range(1,n+1): 
    f *=k
s =0
for k in range(1,n+1):
    s += f//k
print(s)

But can we golf it more?

Alternatively:

Shortest code in Python:

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

typedef long long ll;

struct TestCase {
    int n;
    vector<pair<ll, ll>> x;
    vector<pair<ll, ll>> y;
    vector<pair<ll, ll>> z;
};

vector<TestCase> read_input() {
    int t;
    cin >> t;
    vector<TestCase> cases(t);
    for (int i = 0; i < t; ++i) {
        TestCase& tc = cases[i];
        cin >> tc.n;
        tc.x.resize(tc.n);
        for (int j = 0; j < tc.n; ++j)
            cin >> tc.x[j].first >> tc.x[j].second;
        tc.y.resize(tc.n - 1);
        for (int j = 0; j < tc.n - 1; ++j)
            cin >> tc.y[j].first >> tc.y[j].second;
        tc.z.resize(tc.n - 2);
        for (int j = 0; j < tc.n - 2; ++j)
            cin >> tc.z[j].first >> tc.z[j].second;
    }
    return cases;
}

void solve() {
    vector<TestCase> cases = read_input();
    for (const auto& tc : cases) {
        int n = tc.n;
        const auto& x = tc.x;
        const auto& y = tc.y;
        const auto& z = tc.z;

        if (n < 3) {
            cout << "NO\n";
            continue;
        }

        ll delta_low = y[0].first;
        ll delta_high = y[0].second;
        if (delta_low > delta_high) {
            cout << "NO\n";
            continue;
        }

        vector<double> s_min(n + 1), s_max(n + 1);
        s_min[1] = 0.0;
        s_max[1] = 0.0;
        s_min[2] = delta_low;
        s_max[2] = delta_high;

        bool possible = true;
        for (int i = 3; i <= n; ++i) {
            int y_idx = i - 2;
            int z_idx = i - 3;
            if (y_idx >= (int)y.size() || z_idx >= (int)z.size()) {
                possible = false;
                break;
            }

            ll y_lo = y[y_idx].first;
            ll y_hi = y[y_idx].second;
            ll z_lo = z[z_idx].first;
            ll z_hi = z[z_idx].second;

            ll new_lo = max(y_lo, delta_low + z_lo);
            ll new_hi = min(y_hi, delta_high + z_hi);
            if (new_lo > new_hi) {
                possible = false;
                break;
            }

            delta_low = new_lo;
            delta_high = new_hi;
            s_min[i] = s_min[i - 1] + delta_low;
            s_max[i] = s_max[i - 1] + delta_high;
        }

        if (!possible) {
            cout << "NO\n";
            continue;
        }

        double global_lo = -1e18;
        double global_hi = 1e18;
        for (int k = 1; k <= n; ++k) {
            double curr_lo, curr_hi;
            if (k == 1) {
                curr_lo = x[k - 1].first;
                curr_hi = x[k - 1].second;
            } else {
                curr_lo = x[k - 1].first - s_max[k];
                curr_hi = x[k - 1].second - s_min[k];
            }
            global_lo = max(global_lo, curr_lo);
            global_hi = min(global_hi, curr_hi);
            if (global_lo > global_hi) {
                possible = false;
                break;
            }
        }

        cout << (possible && global_lo <= global_hi ? "YES\n" : "NO\n");
    }
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    solve();
    return 0;
}<#sep#> is 111. So, instead of messing with digits, the problem is equivalent to N and N + 111 both being perfect squares, with N a three-digit number. So, we need to find three-digit squares N where N + 111 is also a square.

Wow, that's a key insight. This simplifies the problem a lot. So instead of checking each digit, just check if N + 111 is also a square.

So, the problem reduces to finding x² and y² such that y² - x² = 111, where x² is a three-digit number. Since N is three-digit, x is between 10 and 31, as we established before. So, we need y² - x² = 111, so (y - x)(y + x) = 111.

Now, 111 factors into primes as 3*37. So, the factors of 111 are 1*111, 3*37. Since y and x are integers, y - x and y + x must be positive integers and factors of 111. Also, y + x > y - x, and both factors have the same parity since y and x are integers (their sum and difference must both be even or both be odd). Let's check the factor pairs:

1 and 111: 1*111. Then y - x = 1, y + x = 111. Solving these equations:

Adding both equations: 2y = 112 => y = 56. Then x = 56 - 1 = 55. But x has to be between 10 and 31. So x = 55 is outside the range. Disregard.

Next factor pair: 3 and 37. So y - x = 3, y + x = 37. Adding equations: 2y = 40 => y = 20. Then x = 20 - 3 = 17. So x = 17. Now, check if x² is a three-digit number. 17² = 289. Wait, but 289 is a three-digit number, yes. Then y = 20, y² = 400. So, 289 + 111 = 400. Which is correct. So 289 is a candidate.

But wait, when we did the digit check earlier, 289 was excluded because it has a 9. But according to this equation, it's a solution. Wait, there's a conflict here. Let's check why.

Earlier, we excluded numbers with any digit 9. Because adding 1 to a digit 9 would make it 10, leading to an invalid digit. But if N is 289, then adding 1 to each digit would give 2→3, 8→9, 9→10. But wait, the units digit becomes 10, which is two digits. So the new number would actually be 390, but that's not a three-digit number. Wait, this seems contradictory. Wait, but according to the equation, N + 111 = 289 + 111 = 400. But 400 is 4,0,0. How does adding 1 to each digit of 289 become 400? Wait, 2 +1 = 3, 8 +1 =9, 9 +1=10. So, the hundreds digit becomes 3, the tens becomes 9, and the units becomes 10. But 10 is two digits, which would make the number 3 (hundreds), 9 (tens), 10 (units), which is 3*100 + 9*10 +10 = 300 +90 +10 = 400. So, technically, even though the units digit overflows, it still results in a valid number, 400, because the 10 in the units digit would carry over. But this contradicts our initial assumption that digits cannot be 9 because adding 1 would lead to two digits. Wait, but in reality, when adding 1 to each digit in the number 289, you have to handle the carry-over. If a digit is 9, adding 1 would make it 10, which is a carry-over to the next digit. So this complicates things. The previous approach of simply adding 111 is only valid if there are no carry-overs when incrementing each digit by 1. Because if there is a carry-over, then the total addition isn't simply 111.

Ah, here's the mistake! My initial assumption that adding 1 to each digit results in N + 111 is only true if there are no carry-overs. If a digit is 9, adding 1 would cause a carry-over, which would add more than 111. Therefore, the equation y² - x² = 111 only works if none of the digits in x² are 9. But in this case, x² = 289 has a 9 in the units digit, so adding 1 would lead to a carry-over. Therefore, the equation N + 111 = y² is invalid here because the actual number after adding 1 to each digit with a carry-over is different.

So this is a problem. Therefore, the previous approach is incorrect because it doesn't account for carry-over when digits are 9. Therefore, we have to discard that method and go back to the original approach of checking each three-digit square without any 9s and see if adding 1 to each digit (without carry-over) results in another square.

Wait, but the problem states "if each digit is increased by one". So, does that mean that each digit is individually increased by 1, without considering carry-over? For example, 289 would become 2+1=3, 8+1=9, 9+1=10, resulting in 3,9,10 which is not a valid three-digit number. So such a case would be invalid. Therefore, the original number must not have any digit as 9, so that when you add 1 to each digit, there's no carry-over, and the result is a three-digit number where each digit is one more than the original. So in that case, the equation N + 111 = y² is valid, because there's no carry-over. Therefore, in that case, solving y² - x² = 111 with the added constraint that x² has no digits of 9. But earlier, when we considered x=17, x²=289, which has a 9, so that's invalid. Therefore, the solution x=17 is invalid because 289 has a 9. So the previous solution is invalid.

So in reality, we need to find x such that x² is a three-digit number with no digits 9, and y² - x² = 111. But since x² must not have a 9, the possible x values are those whose squares don't have 9 in any digit.

So, combining both conditions: x² is a three-digit number with no digits 9, and y² = x² + 111 is a perfect square.

So, let's revisit the list of three-digit squares without any 9s, which we had as 15 numbers:

100, 121, 144, 225, 256, 324, 361, 400, 441, 484, 576, 625, 676, 784, 841.

Now, for each of these, compute x² + 111 and check if it's a perfect square.

Let's go through each:

1. x² = 100. 100 + 111 = 211. Is 211 a square? No. Between 14²=196 and 15²=225.

2. x² = 121. 121 + 111 = 232. 232 is not a square.

3. x² = 144. 144 + 111 = 255. Not a square.

4. x² = 225. 225 + 111 = 336. Not a square.

5. x² = 256. 256 + 111 = 367. Not a square.

6. x² = 324. 324 + 111 = 435. Not a square.

7. x² = 361. 361 + 111 = 472. Not a square.

8. x² = 400. 400 + 111 = 511. Not a square.

9. x² = 441. 441 + 111 = 552. Not a square.

10. x² = 484. 484 + 111 = 595. Not a square.

11. x² = 576. 576 + 111 = 687. Not a square.

12. x² = 625. 625 + 111 = 736. Not a square.

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <algorithm>
#include <iostream>

using namespace std;

vector<int> compute_prefix_function(const string &s) {
    int n = s.size();
    vector<int> pi(n, 0);
    for (int i = 1; i < n; ++i) {
        int j = pi[i-1];
        while (j > 0 && s[i] != s[j]) {
            j = pi[j-1];
        }
        if (s[i] == s[j]) {
            j++;
        }
        pi[i] = j;
    }
    return pi;
}

int get_next_state(int state, char c, const string &s, const vector<int> &pi) {
    if (state == s.size()) {
        state = pi[state - 1];
    }
    while (state > 0 && s[state] != c) {
        state = pi[state - 1];
    }
    if (state < s.size() && s[state] == c) {
        state++;
    } else {
        state = 0;
    }
    return state;
}

vector<vector<int>> build_transition(const string &s) {
    int n = s.size();
    vector<int> pi = compute_prefix_function(s);
    vector<vector<int>> trans(n + 1, vector<int>(26, 0));
    for (int state = 0; state <= n; ++state) {
        for (char c = 'a'; c <= 'z'; ++c) {
            trans[state][c - 'a'] = get_next_state(state, c, s, pi);
        }
    }
    return trans;
}

int main() {
    string c, s, t;
    cin >> c >> s >> t;

    auto trans_s = build_transition(s);
    auto trans_t = build_transition(t);
    int len_s = s.size();
    int len_t = t.size();

    vector<vector<int>> current_dp(len_s + 1, vector<int>(len_t + 1, -1e9));
    current_dp[0][0] = 0;

    for (char curr_char : c) {
        vector<vector<int>> next_dp(len_s + 1, vector<int>(len_t + 1, -1e9));
        for (int a = 0; a <= len_s; ++a) {
            for (int b = 0; b <= len_t; ++b) {
                if (current_dp[a][b] == -1e9) continue;
                int current_val = current_dp[a][b];
                vector<char> chars;
                if (curr_char == '*') {
                    for (char ch = 'a'; ch <= 'z'; ++ch) chars.push_back(ch);
                } else {
                    chars.push_back(curr_char);
                }
                for (char ch : chars) {
                    int a_new = trans_s[a][ch - 'a'];
                    int delta_s = (a_new == len_s) ? 1 : 0;

                    int b_new = trans_t[b][ch - 'a'];
                    int delta_t = (b_new == len_t) ? 1 : 0;

                    int new_val = current_val + delta_s - delta_t;
                    if (new_val > next_dp[a_new][b_new]) {
                        next_dp[a_new][b_new] = new_val;
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        current_dp = move(next_dp);
    }

    int ans = -1e9;
    for (int a = 0; a <= len_s; ++a)
        for (int b = 0; b <= len_t; ++b)
            ans = max(ans, current_dp[a][b]);

    cout << ans << endl;
    return 0;

8. x² = 400. 400 + 111 = 511. Not a square.

9. x² = 441. 441 + 111 = 552. Not a square.

10. x² = 484. 484 + 111 = 595. Not a square.

11. x² = 576. 576 + 111 = 687. Not a square.

14. x² = 784. 784 + 111 = 895. Not a square.

15. x² = 841. 841 + 111 = 952. Not a square.

So according to this, none of them satisfy the condition. So the answer should be 0?

But that seems counterintuitive. There's no such numbers?

Wait, but wait, when we thought of 289 + 111 = 400, which would correspond to 289→400 via digit increments, but 289 has a 9, so it's invalid. So the problem is that even though mathematically N + 111 works, the digit constraint invalidates it. Therefore, the answer is indeed zero?

But let me check if there is any other case. Maybe when there are carry-overs, but the problem statement allows that? Wait, the problem says "if each digit is increased by one". If they mean strictly increasing each digit by one regardless of carry-over, then even numbers with 9 digits would be allowed, but in that case, the resulting number would have more digits. But since we need the result to also be a three-digit number, the original number cannot have any digit as 9, because adding 1 would make it two digits. For example, 199 would become 2100, which is four digits, invalid. So the problem implicitly requires that the resulting number is also a three-digit number. Therefore, the original number cannot have any digit as 9. Therefore, the only way that the resulting number is three-digit is that each digit in the original is at most 8. Therefore, carry-over is not allowed. Therefore, the equation N + 111 = y² must hold, and N must have digits only up to 8.

But in that case, since none of the N's with no 9 digits satisfy N + 111 being a square, the answer is zero. Hmm.

But maybe there's a mistake in this reasoning. Let me think of another approach. Let's consider all possible three-digit squares without 9s and check if their digit-incremented versions are squares.

Take for example N = 121. Digit increments: 232. Not a square.

N = 484. Increment digits: 595. 595 is not a square.

N = 676. Increment digits: 787. 787 is not a square.

N = 576. Increment digits: 687. Not a square.

N = 225. Increment digits: 336. Not a square.

N = 400. Increment digits: 511. Not a square.

N = 100. Increment digits: 211. Not a square.

N = 625. Increment digits: 736. Not a square.

N = 256. Increment digits: 367. Not a square.

N = 324. Increment digits: 435. Not a square.

N = 361. Increment digits: 472. Not a square.

N = 441. Increment digits: 552. Not a square.

N = 784. Increment digits: 895. Not a square.

N = 841. Increment digits: 952. Not a square.

N = 144. Increment digits: 255. Not a square.

N = 400. Increment digits: 511. Not a square.

So indeed, none of them work. Therefore, the answer should be zero.

But wait, let me check if I maybe made a mistake in calculating the digit increments. Let's check N = 121 again. 1+1=2, 2+1=3, 1+1=2. So 232. 232. Let's check 15²=225, 16²=256. 232 is in between. Not a square.

N = 484. 4+1=5, 8+1=9, 4+1=5. So 595. 24²=576, 25²=625. 595 is between. Not a square.

N = 784. 7+1=8, 8+1=9, 4+1=5. So 895. 29²=841, 30²=900. 895 is between. Not a square.

N = 361. 3+1=4, 6+1=7, 1+1=2. 472. 21²=441, 22²=484. Not a square.

Hmm. Maybe there is no such number. Therefore, the answer is zero.

So, there are no solutions where N is a three-digit square with no 9s such that N + 111 is also a square. Hence, the answer is zero.

Therefore, the number of such three-digit perfect squares is zero. So the final answer is \boxed{0}.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{0}

\[100, 121, 144, 225, 256, 324, 361, 400, 441, 484, 576, 625, 676, 784, 841\]

1. \(100 + 111 = 211\) (not a perfect square)
2. \(121 + 111 = 232\) (not a perfect square)
3. \(144 + 111 = 255\) (not a perfect<#sep#>human: Alice thinks of a non-decreasing sequence of non-negative integers and wants Bob to guess it by providing him the set of all its K-sums with repetitions. 

What is this? Let the sequence be {A[1], A[2], ..., A[N]} and K be some positive integer that both Alice and Bob know. Alice gives Bob the set of all possible values that can be genereated by this - A[i_{1}] + A[i_{2}] + ... + A[i_{K}], where 1 ≤ i_{1} ≤ i_{2} ≤ ... ≤ i_{K} ≤ N. She can provide the values generated in any order she wishes to. Bob's task is to restore the initial sequence.

Consider an example. Let N = 3 and K = 2. The sequence is {A[1], A[2], A[3]}. The sequence of its 2-sums with repetitions is {A[1] + A[1], A[1] + A[2], A[1] + A[3], A[2] + A[2], A[2] + A[3], A[3] + A[3]}. But its elements could be provided in any order. For example any permutation of {2, 3, 4, 4, 5, 6} corresponds to the sequence {1, 2, 3}.

Input Format

The first line of the input contains an integer T denoting the number of test cases. 

The description of T test cases follows. 

The first line of each test case contains two space separated integers N and K. 

The second line contains the sequence S_{i} of all K-sums with repetitions of the sequence Alice initially thought of.

Constraints

$1\leq T\leq10^5$  
$1\leq N\leq10^5$  
$1\leq K\leq10^9$  
$2\leq S_i\leq10^{18}$  

Note 

The total number of elements in any input sequence does not exceed 10^{5} 

Each element of each input sequence is non-negative integer not exceeding 10^{18}. 

Each input sequence is a correct sequence of all K-sums with repetitions of some non-decreasing sequence of non-negative integers.

Output Format

For each test case, output a single line containing the space separated list of elements of the non-decreasing sequence Alice thinks of. If there are several possible outputs you can output any of them.

Sample Input 0
3
1 3
3
2 2
12 34 56
3 2
2 3 4 4 5 6

Sample Output 0
1
6 28
1 2 3

Explanation 0

Sample case #01: Since 6 + 6 = 12, 6 + 28 = 34, 28 + 28 =
56, then Alice indeed could think of the sequence {6, 28}.

Sample case #02: It corresponds to the example in the problem
statement.

+@py_random_state(-1)
+def df_params_generator(number_of_facilities, number_of_tasks, seed=0):
+    """
+    Generate instance parameters for the df problem set mentioned in [1].
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    number_of_facilities: int
+        the number of facilities to schedule on
+    number_of_tasks: int
+        the number of tasks to assign to facilities
+    seed: int, random object or None
+        for randomization
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    number_of_facilities: int
+        the number of facilities to schedule on
+    number_of_tasks: int
+        the number of tasks to assign to facilities
+    processing_times: dict[(int,int),int]
+        time steps to process each task
+    capacities: list[int]
+        capacity of each facility
+    assignment_costs: dict[(int,int),int]
+        cost of assigning a task to a facility
+    release_times: list[int]
+        time step at which a job is released
+    deadlines: dict[int, int]
+        deadline (time step) to finish a job
+    resource_requirements: dict[(int,int),int]
+        resources required for each task assigned to a facility
+
+    References
+    ----------
+    ..[1] http://public.tepper.cmu.edu/jnh/instances.htm
+    """
+    capacities, resource_requirements = _common_hooker_params(
+        number_of_facilities, number_of_tasks, seed
+    )
+
+    release_dates = [0] * number_of_tasks
+
+    random_release_time = seed.choice(release_dates)
+    due_dates = [
+        seed.randrange(
+            random_release_time
+            + _due_date_helper(1 / 4 * 1 / 2, number_of_facilities, number_of_tasks),
+            random_release_time
+            + _due_date_helper(1 / 2, number_of_facilities, number_of_tasks),
+        )
+        for _ in range(number_of_tasks)
+    ]
+
+    processing_times = {}
+    range_start = 2 if number_of_facilities <= 20 else 5  # P1 in the reference website
+    for i in range(number_of_facilities):
+        for j in range(number_of_tasks):
+            processing_times[j, i] = seed.randrange(range_start, 30 - i * 5)
+
+    processing_costs = {}
+    for i in range(number_of_facilities):
+        for j in range(number_of_tasks):
+            processing_costs[j, i] = seed.randrange(10 + 10 * i, 40 + 10 * i)
+
+    return (
+        number_of_facilities,
+        number_of_tasks,
+        processing_times,
+        capacities,
+        processing_costs,
+        release_dates,
+        due_dates,
+        resource_requirements,
+    )
+
+
+def _due_date_helper(a, number_of_facilities, number_of_tasks):
+    return math.ceil(
+        5 * a * number_of_tasks * (number_of_facilities + 1) / number_of_facilities
+    )

+def expand_parameters(function, **parameter_lists):
+    """
+    Calls a function with every combination of params
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    function: function
+    parameter_lists: dict[str,list]
+        Maps parameter name to all values it might take
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    generator: Generator
+        Generator of returned values from function with each parameter combination
+    """
+    parameter_names = parameter_lists.keys()
+    all_possible_parameters = itertools.product(*parameter_lists.values())
+    for params in all_possible_parameters:
+        yield function(**{name: val for name, val in zip(parameter_names, params)})

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <queue>
#include <set>
using namespace std;

const int dx[] = {1, -1, 0, 0};
const int dy[] = {0, 0, 1, -1};

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    int n, m;
    cin >> n >> m;

    vector<string> grid(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        cin >> grid[i];
    }

    vector<vector<bool>> visited_border(n, vector<bool>(m, false));
    queue<pair<int, int>> q;

    // Mark 0s connected to the border
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            if (i == 0 || i == n-1 || j == 0 || j == m-1) {
                if (grid[i][j] == '0' && !visited_border[i][j]) {
                    q.push({i, j});
                    visited_border[i][j] = true;
                }
            }
        }
    }

    while (!q.empty()) {
        auto [x, y] = q.front();
        q.pop();

        for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) {
            int nx = x + dx[d];
            int ny = y + dy[d];
            if (nx >= 0 && nx < n && ny >= 0 && ny < m) {
                if (grid[nx][ny] == '0' && !visited_border[nx][ny]) {
                    visited_border[nx][ny] = true;
                    q.push({nx, ny});
                }
            }
        }
    }

    int max_length = 0;

    // Find all holes
    vector<vector<bool>> visited_hole(n, vector<bool>(m, false));
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        for (int j = 0; j < m; ++j) {
            if (grid[i][j] == '0' && !visited_border[i][j] && !visited_hole[i][j]) {
                // Found a new hole
                queue<pair<int, int>> hole_q;
                hole_q.push({i, j});
                visited_hole[i][j] = true;
                vector<pair<int, int>> hole_cells;

                while (!hole_q.empty()) {
                    auto [x, y] = hole_q.front();
                    hole_q.pop();
                    hole_cells.push_back({x, y});

                    for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) {
                        int nx = x + dx[d];
                        int ny = y + dy[d];
                        if (nx >= 0 && nx < n && ny >= 0 && ny < m) {
                            if (grid[nx][ny] == '0' && !visited_border[nx][ny] && !visited_hole[nx][ny]) {
                                visited_hole[nx][ny] = true;
                                hole_q.push({nx, ny});
                            }
                        }
                    }
                }

                // Collect contour
                vector<vector<bool>> contour(n, vector<bool>(m, false));
                for (auto [x, y] : hole_cells) {
                    for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) {
                        int nx = x + dx[d];
                        int ny = y + dy[d];
                        if (nx >= 0 && nx < n && ny >= 0 && ny < m && grid[nx][ny] == '1') {
                            contour[nx][ny] = true;
                        }
                    }
                }

                // Check if contour is a valid cycle
                bool valid = true;
                int count = 0;
                for (int x = 0; x < n && valid; ++x) {
                    for (int y = 0; y < m && valid; ++y) {
                        if (contour[x][y]) {
                            count++;
                            int neighbors = 0;
                            for (int d = 0; d < 4; ++d) {
                                int nx = x + dx[d];
                                int ny = y + dy[d];
                                if (nx >= 0 && nx < n && ny >= 0 && ny < m && contour[nx][ny]) {
                                    neighbors++;
                                }
                            }
                            if (neighbors != 2) {
                                valid = false;
                            }
                        }
                    }
                }

                if (valid && count >= 2) {
                    max_length = max(max_length, count);
                }
            }
        }
    }

    cout << max_length << endl;

    return 0;
}<#sep#>576, 625, 676, 784, 841\]

Next, we check if increasing each digit of these numbers by 1 results in another perfect square. Increasing each digit by 1 is equivalent to adding 111 to the original number. We need to verify if \(N + 111\) is a perfect square for each candidate \(N\):

1. \(100 + 111 = 211\) (not a perfect square)
2. \(121 + 111 = 232\) (not a perfect square)
3. \(144 + 111 = 255\) (not a perfect square)
4. \(225 + 111 = 336\) (not a perfect square)
5. \(256 + 111 = 367\) (not a perfect square)
6. \(324 + 111 = 435\) (not a perfect square)
7. \(361 + 111 = 472\) (not a perfect square)
8. \(400 + 111 = 511\) (not a perfect square)
9. \(441 + 111 = 552\) (not a perfect square)
10. \(484 + 111 = 595\) (not a perfect square)
11. \(576 + 111 = 687\) (not a perfect square)
12. \(625 + 111 = 736\) (not a perfect square)
13. \(676 + 111 = 787\) (not a perfect square)
14. \(784 + 111 = 895\) (not a perfect square)
15. \(841 + 111 = 952\) (not a perfect square)

None of these results are perfect squares. Additionally, checking the factor pairs of 111 confirms that the only mathematical solution \(y^2 - x^2 = 111\) involves numbers with a digit 9, which is invalid.

Thus, there are no three-digit perfect squares that satisfy the given condition. The final answer is:

+@py_random_state(3)
+def cornuejols_instance_params(n_customers, n_facilities, ratio, seed):
+    # locations for customers
+    c_x = np.array([seed.random() for _ in range(n_customers)])
+    c_y = np.array([seed.random() for _ in range(n_customers)])
+

+    f_x = np.array([seed.random() for _ in range(n_facilities)])
+    f_y = np.array([seed.random() for _ in range(n_facilities)])
+
+    demands = np.array(seed.sample(range(5, 35 + 1), k=n_customers))
+    capacities = np.array(seed.sample(range(10, 160 + 1), k=n_facilities))
+    fixed_costs = np.array(seed.sample(range(100, 110 + 1), k=n_facilities) * np.sqrt(capacities)) + np.array(
+        seed.sample(range(90 + 1), k=n_facilities))
+    fixed_costs = fixed_costs.astype(int)
+
+    # adjust capacities according to ratio
+    total_demand = demands.sum()
+    total_capacity = capacities.sum()
+    capacities = capacities * ratio * total_demand / total_capacity
+    capacities = capacities.astype(int)
+

+        + (c_y.reshape((-1, 1)) - f_y.reshape((1, -1))) ** 2) * 10 * demands.reshape((-1, 1))

• Concept Questions (CQ): Provide a new way of engaging with non-computational questions. Students enter a free response before they choose a multiple-choice answer, closing the gap between homework and test preparedness.
• Labs (Lab): Students can perform real statistical analysis in class or online with premade and chapter-specific Stats Labs. Require students to use the instructor-selected data analysis tool to analyze a real data set - pulling together knowledge learned from that chapter and previous material to facilitate whole-picture learning.
