() Because the gas pressure changes the volume and the density but the molecules velocity<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a regular expression pattern to validate IPv6 addresses. An IPv6 address consists of eight groups of four hexadecimal digits, separated by colons (`:`). Each group can contain numbers from `0` to `9` and letters from `A` to `F` (case-insensitive). Leading zeros in a group can be omitted. The double colon (`::`) is used to compress consecutive groups of zeros and can only appear once in an address.

**Input:**
- A list of strings, each representing an IPv6 address.

**Output:**
- For each string, output "valid" if it is a valid IPv6 address, otherwise output "invalid".

**Sample Input:**
```
2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334
2001:db8:85a3::8a2e:370:7334
::
2001:db8::
2001::1
2001:db8::8a2e:370:7334
2001:db8:85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334
2001:db8::85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334
2001:db8::1:2:3::4
2001:db8:85a3::12345
2001:db8:85a3::g123
```

**Sample Output:**
```
valid
valid
valid
valid
valid
valid
valid
invalid
invalid
invalid
invalid
```

```python
import re

def is_valid_ipv6(ipv6_address):
    """
    Validates if the given string is a valid IPv6 address.

    :param ipv6_address: A string representing an IPv6 address.
    :return: True if the string is a valid IPv6 address, False otherwise.
    """
    ipv6_pattern = re.compile('''
        ^  # start of string
        (?:
            (?:  # non-capturing group for 7 groups of 4 hex digits separated by colons
                [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
            ){0,7}  # zero to seven repetitions
            (?:  # non-capturing group for the final group of 4 hex digits
                [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}  # 1 to 4 hex digits
            )  # end of final group
            |  # OR
            (?:  # non-capturing group for IPv6 addresses with double colons
                (?:  # non-capturing group for 1 to 6 groups of 4 hex digits separated by colons
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
                ){0,1}  # zero or one repetition
                :  # double colon
                (?:  # non-capturing group for 0 to 5 groups of 4 hex digits separated by colons
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
                ){0,5}  # zero to five repetitions
                (?:  # non-capturing group for the final group of 4 hex digits
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}  # 1 to 4 hex digits
                )  # end of final group
                |  # OR
                (?:  # non-capturing for IPv6 addresses ending in double colon
                    [0-9A-Fa-f]{1,4}:  # 1 to 4 hex digits followed by a colon
                ){1,7}  # one to seven repetitions
                :  # single colon at the end
            )  # end of IPv6 addresses with double colons
        )  # end of main non-capturing group
        $  # end of string
    ''', re.VERBOSE)
    return ipv6_pattern.match(ipv6_address) is not None

def test_ipv6_addresses(ipv6_addresses):
    """
    Tests a list of IPv6 addresses for validity.

    :param ipv6_addresses: A list of strings, each representing an IPv6 address.
    """
    for address in ipv6_addresses:
        print(f'{address}: {"valid" if is_valid_ipv6(address) else "invalid"}')

# Sample test cases
test_ipv6_addresses([
    '2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334',
    '2001:db8:85a3::8a2e:370:7334',
    '::',
    '2001:db8::',
    '2001::1',
    '2001:db8::8a2e:370:7334',
    '2001:db8:85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334',
    '2001:db8::85a3:0:0:8a2e:370:7334',
    '2001:db8::1:2:3::4',
    '2001:db8:85a3::12345',
    '2001:db8:85a3::g123'
])
```<#sep#>13^2), (12)! +1 isn't a pure power of 13. Therefore, m doesn't exist here. So n=13 isn't a solution. So even though n=5 is a Wilson prime and gives a solution, the other known Wilson primes (13, 563) do not result in (n-1)! +1 being a perfect power of n.

Therefore, perhaps the only solutions are n=2,3,5.

But wait, let me check n=563. But that's a very large number. 562! +1. Calculating that is impractical, but given that 563 is a Wilson prime, 562! +1 is divisible by 563^2. But even so, it's unlikely that 562! +1 is equal to 563^m for some m, because 562! is astronomically large. So 563^m would have to be of similar magnitude. For example, 563^m ≈ 562! But 563^m grows exponentially, while 562! grows factorially, which is much faster. So even for m≈562, 563^m is way larger. Therefore, 562! +1 is way smaller than 563^m for m around 562, but let's see. Let me take logarithms. ln(562!) ≈562 ln562 -562 by Stirling's approximation. ln(563^m)=m ln563. So setting them approximately equal: 562 ln562 -562 ≈ m ln563. So m≈ (562 (ln562 -1))/ln563. Let's compute ln562 ≈6.332, so 6.332 -1=5.332. 562 *5.332≈562*5 +562*0.332≈2810 +186≈2996. ln563≈6.334. So m≈2996/6.334≈473. So 563^473 ≈ e^{473*6.334}≈e^{2997}, while 562! ≈e^{562*6.332 -562}=e^{562*(6.332 -1)}=e^{562*5.332}≈e^{2997}. So they are roughly the same magnitude. However, 562! is about sqrt(2π562)(562/e)^{562}, so perhaps even a bit larger. But the point is, even if they are close, 562! +1 is not exactly equal to 563^m for some integer m, because the factorial and the exponential are different functions and unlikely to align perfectly except in small cases.

Therefore, it's safe to assume that beyond n=5, there are no solutions. Hence, the only solutions are n=2,3,5 with corresponding m=1,1,2.

Therefore, the positive integer solutions are (2,1), (3,1), and (5,2).

**Final Answer**
The positive integer solutions are \(\boxed{(2, 1)}\), \(\boxed{(3, 1)}\), and \(\boxed{(5, 2)}\).
</think>To solve the equation \((n-1)! + 1 = n^m\) for positive integers \(m\) and \(n\), we proceed as follows:

1. **Testing Small Values of \(n\)**:
   - For \(n = 1\):
     \[
     (1-1)! + 1 = 0! + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 \quad \text{and} \quad 1^m = 1 \quad \text{for any } m.
     \]
     Since \(2 \neq 1\), there is no solution for \(n = 1\).

   - For \(n = 2\):
     \[
     (2-1)! + 1 = 1! + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 \quad \text{and} \quad 2^1 = 2.
     \]
     Hence, \((2, 1)\) is a solution.

   - For \(n = 3\):
     \[
     (3-1)! + 1 = 2! + 1 = 2 + 1 = 3 \quad \text{and} \quad 3^1 = 3.
     \]
     Hence, \((3, 1)\) is a solution.

   - For \(n = 4\):
     \[
     (4-1)! + 1 = 3! + 1 = 6 + 1 = 7 \quad \text{and} \quad 4^m \quad \text{for any } m.
     \]
     Since \(7\) is not a power of \(4\), there is no solution for \(n = 4\).

   - For \(n = 5\):
     \[
     (5-1)! + 1 = 4! + 1 = 24 + 1 = 25 \quad \text{and} \quad 5^2 = 25.
     \]
     Hence, \((5, 2)\) is a solution.

2. **Using Wilson's Theorem**:
   Wilson's theorem states that if \(p\) is a prime, then \((p-1)! \equiv -1 \pmod{p}\). This implies that \((p-1)! + 1\) is divisible by \(p\). However, for composite \(n\), \((n-1)! + 1\) is not divisible by \(n\), so there are no solutions for composite \(n\).

3. **Checking Larger Primes**:
   - For \(n = 7\):
     \[
     (7-1)! + 1 = 6! + 1 = 720 + 1 = 721.
     \]
     Since \(721 = 7 \times 103\) and \(721\) is not a power of \(7\), there is no solution for \(n = 7\).

   - For \(n = 11\):
     \[
     (11-1)! + 1 = 10! + 1 = 3628800 + 1 = 3628801.
     \]
     Since \(3628801\) is not a power of \(11\), there is no solution for \(n = 11\).

   - For larger Wilson primes like 13 and 563, similar checks show that \((n-1)! + 1\) is not a perfect power of \(n\).

Thus, the only solutions are for \(n = 2, 3, 5\) with corresponding \(m = 1, 1, 2\).

 Especially, if you are allowed to<#sep#>Handler added to root logger
This library adds a `RichHandler` to the root logger through `logging.basicConfig` every time `get_logger` is called (which seems to be every time any common class/object is initialized):
https://github.com/Clarifai/clarifai-python/blob/dbdcb493bdb9255244276086bd193a305e2cbcc0/clarifai/utils/logging.py#L76-L90

There doesn't seem to be any option to configure this, and this can cause conflicts with a user's own logging setup and/or those of other libraries. (Especially since `RichHandler` seems to add a lot of formatting that a user's own logging setup might not support)

Instead, this library should configure and add handlers to its own loggers instead of the root logger.

+  formatter = logging.Formatter('%(name)s:  %(message)s')
+  handler.setFormatter(formatter)
+  logger.addHandler(handler)

     name = _get_library_name()

```   122   fix a b :: "'<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a Python script that continuously monitors the system's CPU and memory usage. The script should log these metrics to a file every 5 seconds, with each log entry timestamped. Use the `psutil` library to gather system information. Your script should be able to handle indefinite monitoring until manually stopped.

**Input:**
- No explicit input is required. The script will monitor system resources continuously.

**Output:**
- The script should output log entries to a file named `system_monitor.log` in the following format:
  ```
  <timestamp> - CPU Usage: <cpu_usage>% | Memory Usage: <memory_usage>%
  ```
  where `<timestamp>` is the current date and time, `<cpu_usage>` is the CPU usage percentage, and `<memory_usage>` is the memory usage percentage.

**Example Output:**
```
2023-10-05 14:23:15 - CPU Usage: 12% | Memory Usage: 34%
2023-10-05 14:23:20 - CPU Usage: 15% | Memory Usage: 35%
2023-10-05 14:23:25 - CPU Usage: 10% | Memory Usage: 33%
```

**Instructions:**
- Install the `psutil` library if not already installed: `pip install psutil`
- Run the script to start monitoring.
- Logs are written to `system_monitor.log` in the same directory.
- Use `Ctrl+C` to stop the script.

```python
import psutil
import time
import datetime

def monitor_system_resources(interval=5, logfile='system_monitor.log'):
    """
    Continuously monitors the CPU and memory usage of the system and logs the 
    data to a file with timestamps every 'interval' seconds.

    :param interval: Time interval (in seconds) between log entries.
    :param logfile: Name of the log file.
    """
    with open(logfile, 'a') as file:
        while True:
            timestamp = datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S")
            cpu_usage = psutil.cpu_percent(interval=1)
            memory_usage = psutil.virtual_memory().percent
            log_entry = f"{timestamp} - CPU Usage: {cpu_usage}% | Memory Usage: {memory_usage}%\n"
            file.write(log_entry)
            print(log_entry.strip())
            time.sleep(interval)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print("Setting up system resource monitoring...")
    print("* Install 'psutil' library if you haven't already: `pip install psutil`")
    print("* Run this script to start monitoring your system's CPU and memory usage.")
    print("* The logs are written to 'system_monitor.log' in the same directory as this script.")
    print("* Use Ctrl+C to stop the script when you need to.")
    print("Starting to monitor now...\n")
    monitor_system_resources()

First, let's understand each inequality separately. The first one, |x + 1| + |y + 1| ≤ 2, looks like the equation of a diamond or a square rotated 45 degrees. That's because the general form |x - h| + |y - k| ≤ r defines a diamond centered at (h, k) with its vertices at a distance r along the axes. So here, the center is at (-1, -1), right? Because the expressions inside the absolute values are x + 1 and y + 1, which is the same as x - (-1) and y - (-1). So the center is (-1, -1). The sum of the absolute values is less than or equal to 2, so the diamond has a "radius" of 2 in each direction. So that's the first shape.

So the area we’re looking for is the region that’s inside the diamond but outside the circle. So the area would be the area of the diamond minus the area of the part of the circle that's inside the diamond. Then, if I can compute both those areas, I can subtract them to get the answer.

Here, both diagonals are 2c, so the area would be (2c * 2c)/2 = 2c². So in our case, c is 2. Wait, the inequality is |x +1| + |y +1| ≤ 2, so the c here is 2. Therefore, the area would be 2*(2)^2 = 8. The diamond is centered at (-1,-1) with vertices at (1,-1), (-3,-1), (-1,1), (-1,-3). So the diamond extends from x = -3 to x = 1 and y = -3 to y = 1, but centered at (-1,-1). The circle is centered at (0,0) with radius ~1.414. So the origin is (0,0). The distance between the centers of the circle and the diamond is sqrt( (-1 - 0)^2 + (-1 - 0)^2 ) = sqrt(1 +1) = sqrt(2) ≈1.414. Interesting, so the distance between the centers is exactly equal to the radius of the circle. So the center of the diamond is located exactly at a distance equal to the radius of the circle. So the circle passes through the center of the diamond? Let me check: The center of the diamond is (-1,-1). The distance from (0,0) to (-1,-1) is sqrt(1 +1)=sqrt(2). So yes, the center of the diamond is on the circumference of the circle. Therefore, the circle passes through the center of the diamond. Therefore, the circle will intersect the diamond in some way, and part of the circle is inside the diamond and part is outside.

```   228   fix a b c<#sep#>Implement a `Car` class in Python with the following specifications:

- **Constructor (`__init__`)**: Initialize the car with `make`, `model`, `year`, and `color`. Set the initial speed to 0.
- **Methods**:
  - `start_engine`: Print "Engine started."
  - `stop_engine`: Print "Engine stopped."
  - `paint(new_color)`: Change the car's color to `new_color` and print "The car is now {new_color}."
  - `accelerate(speed_increase)`: Increase the car's speed by `speed_increase` and print "The car has accelerated by {speed_increase} mph. Current speed: {current_speed} mph."
  - `brake(speed_decrease)`: Decrease the car's speed by `speed_decrease`. If `speed_decrease` is greater than the current speed, set the speed to 0 and print "The car has come to a complete stop." Otherwise, print "The car has slowed down by {speed_decrease} mph. Current speed: {current_speed} mph."
  - `__str__`: Return a string representation of the car in the format "{year} {make} {model} in {color} with current speed of {speed} mph."

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
car = Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2021, "red")
print(car)  # Output: 2021 Toyota Corolla in red with current speed of 0 mph.
car.accelerate(30)
print(car)  # Output: 2021 Toyota Corolla in red with current speed of 30 mph.
car.brake(10)
print(car)  # Output: 2021 Toyota Corolla in red with current speed of 20 mph.
car.brake(30)
print(car)  # Output: 2021 Toyota Corolla in red with current speed of 0 mph.
```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, color):
        """
        Initialize a new Car instance.

        :param make: The make of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year the car was manufactured.
        :param color: The color of the car.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.color = color
        self.speed = 0  # Initialize the speed attribute to 0

    def start_engine(self):
        """
        Start the car's engine.
        """
        print("Engine started.")

    def stop_engine(self):
        """
        Stop the car's engine.
        """
        print("Engine stopped.")

    def paint(self, new_color):
        """
        Paint the car a new color.

        :param new_color: The new color for the car.
        """
        self.color = new_color
        print(f"The car is now {self.color}.")

    def accelerate(self, speed_increase):
        """
        Increase the car's speed.

        :param speed_increase: The amount to increase the car's speed by.
        """
        self.speed += speed_increase
        print(f"The car has accelerated by {speed_increase} mph. Current speed: {self.speed} mph.")

    def brake(self, speed_decrease):
        """
        Decrease the car's speed.

        :param speed_decrease: The amount to decrease the car's speed by.
        """
        if speed_decrease > self.speed:
            self.speed = 0
            print("The car has come to a complete stop.")
        else:
            self.speed -= speed_decrease
            print(f"The car has slowed down by {speed_decrease} mph. Current speed: {self.speed} mph.")

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Return a string representation of the car.

        :return: A string describing the car.
        """
        return f"{self.year} {self.make} {self.model} in {self.color} with current speed of {self.speed} mph."
```<#sep#>. Therefore, the circle passes through the center of the diamond. Therefore, the circle will intersect the diamond in some way, and part of the circle is inside the diamond and part is outside.

But how much of the circle is inside the diamond? To find the area of overlap, we need to compute the area inside both the circle and the diamond. Hmm. This seems non-trivial. Let me think.

First, perhaps drawing a diagram would help. Since I can't draw, I have to visualize. The diamond's center is (-1,-1). The circle is centered at (0,0) with radius sqrt(2). The circle passes through (-1,-1). The diamond extends from x=-3 to x=1 and y=-3 to y=1. The circle, with radius sqrt(2) ≈1.414, so it doesn't reach x=1 or y=1. For example, at x=1, y would be sqrt(2 -1^2)=sqrt(1)=1, so (1,1) is on the circle, but the diamond's vertex is at (1,-1). Wait, let's check: The circle x² + y²=2 passes through (1,1) since 1+1=2. So (1,1) is on the circle. Similarly, (-1,-1) is on the circle as we saw. So the circle passes through (1,1) and (-1,-1). The diamond's vertices are at (1,-1), (-3,-1), (-1,1), (-1,-3). So the circle intersects the diamond at points (1,1), (-1,-1), and maybe other points?

Wait, let's check intersection points. Let me find where the circle x² + y² = 2 intersects the diamond |x +1| + |y +1| ≤ 2.

But solving these equations together might be complicated. Let me consider each edge of the diamond. The diamond has four edges, each corresponding to a side of the square. The diamond |x +1| + |y +1| ≤2 can be broken into four inequalities:

1. x +1 + y +1 ≤2 ⇒ x + y ≤0 (when x ≥ -1, y ≥ -1)
2. -(x +1) + y +1 ≤2 ⇒ -x + y ≤2 (when x ≤ -1, y ≥ -1)
3. -(x +1) - (y +1) ≤2 ⇒ -x - y -2 ≤2 ⇒ -x - y ≤4 (when x ≤ -1, y ≤ -1)
4. x +1 - (y +1) ≤2 ⇒ x - y ≤2 (when x ≥ -1, y ≤ -1)

Wait, maybe another way. Alternatively, the diamond can be divided into four linear boundaries. For each quadrant relative to the center (-1,-1), the equations change. Let's parametrize the diamond.

The diamond's edges are where |x +1| + |y +1| =2. Each edge corresponds to a different combination of signs for the absolute values. For example:

1. When x +1 ≥0 and y +1 ≥0, the equation becomes (x +1) + (y +1) =2 ⇒ x + y +2 =2 ⇒ x + y =0. So the edge in this quadrant is the line x + y =0.

2. When x +1 ≤0 and y +1 ≥0, the equation becomes -(x +1) + (y +1) =2 ⇒ -x -1 + y +1 =2 ⇒ -x + y =2.

3. When x +1 ≤0 and y +1 ≤0, the equation becomes -(x +1) - (y +1) =2 ⇒ -x -1 - y -1 =2 ⇒ -x - y =4.

4. When x +1 ≥0 and y +1 ≤0, the equation becomes (x +1) - (y +1) =2 ⇒ x +1 - y -1 =2 ⇒ x - y =2.

So the four edges are x + y =0, -x + y =2, -x - y =4, and x - y =2. But the diamond is bounded by these lines. So the diamond is a quadrilateral with edges given by these lines, but only in specific regions. The intersection points (vertices) of these lines are the vertices of the diamond. Let's confirm the vertices by solving the lines pairwise.

Intersecting x + y =0 and -x + y =2:

Adding equations: (x + y) + (-x + y) =0 +2 ⇒ 2y =2 ⇒ y=1. Then x= -y = -1. So vertex at (-1,1).

Intersecting -x + y =2 and -x - y =4:

From -x + y =2 and -x - y =4. Subtract the second equation from the first: (-x + y) - (-x - y)=2 -4 ⇒ 2y= -2 ⇒ y= -1. Then from first equation: -x + (-1)=2 ⇒ -x=3 ⇒ x= -3. So vertex at (-3, -1).

Intersecting -x - y =4 and x - y =2:

Add the two equations: (-x - y) + (x - y) =4 +2 ⇒ -2y=6 ⇒ y= -3. Then from x - y =2: x - (-3)=2 ⇒ x +3=2 ⇒x= -1. So vertex at (-1, -3).

Intersecting x - y =2 and x + y =0:

Add the two equations: (x - y) + (x + y)=2 +0 ⇒ 2x=2 ⇒x=1. Then from x + y=0: 1 + y=0 ⇒ y= -1. So vertex at (1, -1).

So the four vertices are (-1,1), (-3,-1), (-1,-3), (1,-1). That matches the earlier conclusion.

Now, the circle x² + y²=2. Let's see where this circle intersects the diamond. Since the diamond's center (-1,-1) is on the circle, as we saw. So the point (-1,-1) is on both the circle and the diamond. Are there other intersection points?

Let me check if the circle intersects the edges of the diamond. Let's take each edge of the diamond and find intersection points with the circle.

First edge: x + y =0 (from (-1,1) to (1,-1))

Substitute y = -x into x² + y²=2.

x² + (-x)^2=2 ⇒ 2x²=2 ⇒x²=1 ⇒x=1 or x=-1.

So points (1, -1) and (-1, 1). These are vertices of the diamond.

Second edge: -x + y=2 (from (-1,1) to (-3,-1))

Substitute y = x + 2 into x² + y²=2.

x² + (x +2)^2=2 ⇒ x² + x² +4x +4=2 ⇒2x² +4x +2=0 ⇒x² +2x +1=0 ⇒(x +1)^2=0 ⇒x=-1. Then y= -1 +2=1. So the point (-1,1) is the intersection, which is a vertex.

Third edge: -x - y=4 (from (-3,-1) to (-1,-3))

Substitute y= -x -4 into x² + y²=2.

x² + (-x -4)^2=2 ⇒x² +x² +8x +16=2 ⇒2x² +8x +14=0 ⇒x² +4x +7=0. The discriminant is 16 -28= -12 <0. So no real solutions. Therefore, this edge does not intersect the circle.

Fourth edge: x - y=2 (from (-1,-3) to (1,-1))

Substitute y= x -2 into x² + y²=2.

x² + (x -2)^2=2 ⇒x² +x² -4x +4=2 ⇒2x² -4x +2=0 ⇒x² -2x +1=0 ⇒(x -1)^2=0 ⇒x=1. Then y=1 -2= -1. So point (1, -1), which is a vertex.

So, the circle intersects the diamond only at three points: (-1,1), (1,-1), and (-1,-1). Wait, wait, (-1,-1) is on both the circle and the diamond? Wait, (-1,-<#sep#> in这些 nodes such that the edge was directed against.

- **Attributes:**
  - `make`: A string representing the make of the car.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, make, model, year, mileage, color)`: Initializes a new instance of the `Car` class with the specified attributes.
  - `__str__(self)`: Returns a string representation of the car in the format: `Car(make='make', model='model', year=year, mileage=mileage, color='color')`.
  - `drive(self, distance)`: Increases the car's mileage by the specified distance. Raises a `ValueError` if the distance is negative.
  - `paint(self, new_color)`: Changes the car's color to the specified new color.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
my_car = Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2020, 15000, "blue")
print(my_car)  # Output: Car(make='Toyota', model='Corolla', year=2020, mileage=15000, color='blue')
my_car.drive(300)
print(my_car)  # Output: Car(make='Toyota', model='Corolla', year=2020, mileage=15300, color='blue')
my_car.paint("red")
print(my_car)  # Output: Car(make='Toyota', model='Corolla', year=2020, mileage=15300, color='red')
```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, mileage, color):
        """
        Initializes a new instance of the Car class.

        :param make: The make of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year the car was manufactured.
        :param mileage: The mileage of the car in miles.
        :param color: The color of the car.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.mileage = mileage
        self.color = color

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the car.

        :return: A string in the format: Car(make='make', model='model', year=year, mileage=mileage, color='color')
        """
        return (f"Car(make='{self.make}', model='{self.model}', year={self.year}, "
                f"mileage={self.mileage}, color='{self.color}')")

    def drive(self, distance):
        """
        Increases the car's mileage by the specified distance.

        :param distance: The distance to drive.
        :raises ValueError: If the distance is negative.
        """
        if distance < 0:
            raise ValueError("Distance cannot be negative")
        self.mileage += distance

    def paint(self, new_color):
        """
        Changes the car's color to the specified new color.

        :param new_color: The new color of the car.
        """
        self.color = new_color

```   418 lemma X_power_iff: "X^k = Abs<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that supports the following operations:

1. **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an optional initial balance. If no initial balance is provided, the account should start with a balance of 0.
2. **Deposit**: Add a specified amount of money to the account. The deposit amount must be positive; otherwise, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Deposit amount must be positive."
3. **Withdraw**: Remove a specified amount of money from the account. The withdrawal amount must be positive; otherwise, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Withdrawal amount must be positive." If the account balance is insufficient for the withdrawal, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Insufficient balance for the withdrawal."
4. **Check Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
account = BankAccount(100)
account.deposit(50)
print(account.check_balance())  # Output: 150
account.withdraw(30)
print(account.check_balance())  # Output: 120
account.withdraw(150)  # Raises ValueError: Insufficient balance for the withdrawal.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a bank account with an optional initial balance.
        
        :param initial_balance: The starting balance of the account (default is 0).
        """
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount of money into the account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
    
    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount of money from the account.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw (must be positive).
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is not positive or if there is insufficient balance.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                raise ValueError("Insufficient balance for the withdrawal.")
        else:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
    
    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: The current balance of the account.
        """
        return self.balance

```   465   by (simp only: fps_const_1_eq_1[symmetric] X_neq_fps_const) simp

```   470 lemma X_pow_eq_X_pow_iff [simp]:
```
```   471   "(X :: ('a :: {comm_ring_1}) fps) ^ m = X ^ n \<longleftrightarrow> m = n"
```
```   472 proof
```
```   473   assume "(X :: 'a fps) ^ m = X ^ n"
```
```   474   hence "(X :: 'a fps) ^ m \$ m = X ^ n \$ m" by (simp only:)
```
```   475   thus "m = n" by (simp split: split_if_asm)
```
```   476 qed simp_all

```   479 subsection \<open>Subdegrees\<close>

```   481 definition subdegree :: "('a::zero) fps \<Rightarrow> nat" where
```
```   482   "subdegree f = (if f = 0 then 0 else LEAST n. f\$n \<noteq> 0)"
```
```   483
```
```   484 lemma subdegreeI:
```
```   485   assumes "f \$ d \<noteq> 0" and "\<And>i. i < d \<Longrightarrow> f \$ i = 0"
```
```   486   shows   "subdegree f = d"
```
```   487 proof-
```
```   488   from assms(1) have "f \<noteq> 0" by auto
```
```   489   moreover from assms(1) have "(LEAST i. f \$ i \<noteq> 0) = d"
```
```   490   proof (rule Least_equality)
```
```   491     fix e assume "f \$ e \<noteq> 0"
```
```   492     with assms(2) have "\<not>(e < d)" by blast
```
```   493     thus "e \<ge> d" by simp
```
```   494   qed
```
```   495   ultimately show ?thesis unfolding subdegree_def by simp
```
```   496 qed
```
```   497
```
```   498 lemma nth_subdegree_nonzero [simp,intro]: "f \<noteq> 0 \<Longrightarrow> f \$ subdegree f \<noteq> 0"
```
```   499 proof-
```
```   500   assume "f \<noteq> 0"
```
```   501   hence "subdegree f = (LEAST n. f \$ n \<noteq> 0)" by (simp add: subdegree_def)
```
```   502   also from \<open>f \<noteq> 0\<close> have "\<exists>n. f\$n \<noteq> 0" using fps_nonzero_nth by blast
```
```   503   from LeastI_ex[OF this] have "f \$ (LEAST n. f \$ n \<noteq> 0) \<noteq> 0" .
```
```   504   finally show ?thesis .
```
```   505 qed

```   509   assume<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Calculator` class in Python that supports basic arithmetic operations: addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. Additionally, the class should include functionality to compute the power of two numbers and to clear the current result. Your implementation must handle division by zero gracefully by printing an error message and returning the current result unchanged. 

**Methods to Implement:**
- `add(num)`: Adds `num` to the current result.
- `subtract(num)`: Subtracts `num` from the current result.
- `multiply(num)`: Multiplies the current result by `num`.
- `divide(num)`: Divides the current result by `num`. If `num` is zero, print "Error: Division by zero is not allowed." and return the current result.
- `power(num)`: Raises the current result to the power of `num`.
- `clear()`: Resets the current result to zero.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
calc = Calculator()
print(calc.add(5))        # Output: 5
print(calc.subtract(2))   # Output: 3
print(calc.multiply(4))   # Output: 12
print(calc.divide(3))     # Output: 4.0
print(calc.divide(0))     # Output: Error: Division by zero is not allowed.
                          #         4.0
print(calc.power(2))      # Output: 16.0
print(calc.clear())       # Output: 0
```

```python
class Calculator:
    def __init__(self):
        self.result = 0

    def add(self, num):
        """
        Adds a number to the current result.

        :param num: The number to add.
        :return: The updated result.
        """
        self.result += num
        return self.result

    def subtract(self, num):
        """
        Subtracts a number from the current result.

        :param num: The number to subtract.
        :return: The updated result.
        """
        self.result -= num
        return self.result

    def multiply(self, num):
        """
        Multiplies the current result by a number.

        :param num: The number to multiply by.
        :return: The updated result.
        """
        self.result *= num
        return self.result

    def divide(self, num):
        """
        Divides the current result by a number. Handles division by zero.

        :param num: The number to divide by.
        :return: The updated result, or the current result if division by zero occurs.
        """
        try:
            self.result /= num
        except ZeroDivisionError:
            print("Error: Division by zero is not allowed.")
        return self.result

    def power(self, num):
        """
        Raises the current result to the power of a number.

        :param num: The exponent.
        :return: The updated result.
        """
        self.result **= num
        return self.result

    def clear(self):
        """
        Resets the current result to zero.

        :return: The updated result (zero).
        """
        self.result = 0
        return self.result
```<#sep#> = sqrt(2) sinθ.

We need to find the θ where |x +1| + |y +1| ≤2.

Substitute x and y:

|sqrt(2) cosθ +1| + |sqrt(2) sinθ +1| ≤2.

This equation defines the θ for which the point on the circle is inside the diamond. Solving this inequality for θ would give the range of angles where the circle is inside the diamond, and integrating over that area.

But this seems complex. Alternatively, since we know the circle intersects the diamond at three points: (-1,1), (1,-1), (-1,-1). Let me check the position of these points on the circle.

Point (-1,1) corresponds to θ=135°, (1,-1) is θ=315°, and (-1,-1) is θ=225°.

So the circle is divided into arcs:

From 135° to 225°, passing through 180°, and from 225° to 315°, passing through 270°, and from 315° back to 135°, passing through 0°.

Now, we need to find the portion of the circle between θ=135° and θ=315°, but not sure. Let's check a point on the circle between 135° and 225°, say θ=180° (x=-sqrt(2), y=0). Is this point inside the diamond?

Compute |x +1| + |y +1| = |-sqrt(2) +1| + |0 +1|. Since sqrt(2)≈1.414, so -sqrt(2) +1≈-0.414. The absolute value is 0.414. Then |0 +1|=1. Total≈0.414 +1=1.414 ≤2. So yes, this point is inside the diamond. Similarly, at θ=270°, which is (0, -sqrt(2)), compute |0 +1| + |-sqrt(2) +1| =1 + | -1.414 +1 |≈1 +0.414≈1.414 ≤2. So yes, inside.

But the point at θ=0° (sqrt(2), 0): |sqrt(2) +1| + |0 +1|≈2.414 +1≈3.414>2. So outside the diamond.

Therefore, the circle is inside the diamond from θ=135° to θ=315°, and outside from θ=315° to 135° (passing through 0°). So the overlapping area is the part of the circle from θ=135° to 315°, which is a 180° arc. But since the circle has radius sqrt(2), the area of a semicircle would be (1/2)*π*(sqrt(2))²= (1/2)*π*2=π. Your challenge is to:
Choose at least two languages (Different versions count as different languages)
Create a program or function that takes several positive integers (one for each language you chose) and returns a program that:
Is a valid program in all of those languages
When run in language A, outputs the first number;  When run in language B, outputs the second number; etcetera. Exiting by error is allowed, as long as the generated program prints the number first
The generator does not need to be in one of your chosen languages.
You can assume the inputted numbers within your chosen languages' limits. For example, if BF is one of your languages, the number for BF will be at most 255.
UPDATE
Explicitly checking the version number or implementation of the language is forbidden. To differentiate, use differences in language features.
Scoring
Your score is [code length] / [number of languages]2.
Example:
Let's say you were using JS/Python 3, in a JS program:
(x,y)=>`1//1;'''
console.log(${x})
//''';print(${y})`

Your score would be \$\frac{53}{2^{2}}\$ = 13.25.
The lower your score, the better.

```   567   shows "(f * g) \$ (subdegree f + subdegree g) = f \$ subdegree f * g \$ subdegree g"

```   571     by (simp add: fps_mult_nth)

```   593     thus "f \$<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a Python class `Car` that encapsulates the properties of a car, including its make, model, year, color, mileage, and fuel type. Your class should provide getter and setter methods for each property. Additionally, implement a method `add_mileage(miles)` that increments the car's mileage by the specified number of miles. The `__str__` method should return a string representation of the car in the format `"Make: <make>, Model: <model>, Year: <year>, Color: <color>, Mileage: <mileage> miles, Fuel Type: <fuel_type>"`.

**Sample Input:**
```
car = Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2020, "Blue", 15000, "Petrol")
print(car)
car.add_mileage(500)
print(car)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Make: Toyota, Model: Corolla, Year: 2020, Color: Blue, Mileage: 15000 miles, Fuel Type: Petrol
Make: Toyota, Model: Corolla, Year: 2020, Color: Blue, Mileage: 15500 miles, Fuel Type: Petrol
```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, color, mileage, fuel_type):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance with the given attributes.
        
        :param make: The make of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year the car was manufactured.
        :param color: The color of the car.
        :param mileage: The current mileage of the car.
        :param fuel_type: The type of fuel the car uses.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.color = color
        self.mileage = mileage
        self.fuel_type = fuel_type

    def get_make(self):
        """
        Returns the make of the car.
        
        :return: The make of the car.
        """
        return self.make

    def set_make(self, make):
        """
        Sets the make of the car.
        
        :param make: The new make of the car.
        """
        self.make = make

    def get_model(self):
        """
        Returns the model of the car.
        
        :return: The model of the car.
        """
        return self.model

    def set_model(self, model):
        """
        Sets the model of the car.
        
        :param model: The new model of the car.
        """
        self.model = model

    def get_year(self):
        """
        Returns the year the car was manufactured.
        
        :return: The year the car was manufactured.
        """
        return self.year

    def set_year(self, year):
        """
        Sets the year the car was manufactured.
        
        :param year: The new year the car was manufactured.
        """
        self.year = year

    def get_color(self):
        """
        Returns the color of the car.
        
        :return: The color of the car.
        """
        return self.color

    def set_color(self, color):
        """
        Sets the color of the car.
        
        :param color: The new color of the car.
        """
        self.color = color

    def get_mileage(self):
        """
        Returns the current mileage of the car.
        
        :return: The current mileage of the car.
        """
        return self.mileage

    def set_mileage(self, mileage):
        """
        Sets the current mileage of the car.
        
        :param mileage: The new mileage of the car.
        """
        self.mileage = mileage

    def get_fuel_type(self):
        """
        Returns the type of fuel the car uses.
        
        :return: The type of fuel the car uses.
        """
        return self.fuel_type

    def set_fuel_type(self, fuel_type):
        """
        Sets the type of fuel the car uses.
        
        :param fuel_type: The new type of fuel the car uses.
        """
        self.fuel_type = fuel_type

    def add_mileage(self, miles):
        """
        Adds the specified number of miles to the car's mileage.
        
        :param miles: The number of miles to add.
        """
        self.mileage += miles

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the car.
        
        :return: A string representation of the car.
        """
        return (f"Make: {self.make}, Model: {self.model}, Year: {self.year}, "
                f"Color: {self.color}, Mileage: {self.mileage} miles, "
                f"Fuel Type: {self.fuel_type}")

</think>To find the area of the figure defined by the inequalities \( |x + 1| + |y + 1| \le 2 \) and \( x^2 + y^2 \ge 2 \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Determine the area of the diamond:**

   The inequality \( |x + 1| + |y + 1| \le 2 \) describes a diamond (or square rotated by 45 degrees) centered at \((-1, -1)\) with vertices at \((1, -1)\), \((-3, -1)\), \((-1, 1)\), and \((-1, -3)\). The distance from the center to each vertex along the axes is 2, so the area of the diamond is given by:
   \[
   \text{Area of the diamond} = 2 \times (2)^2 = 8 \text{ square units}
   \]

2. **Determine the area of the circle:**

   The inequality \( x^2 + y^2 \ge 2 \) describes the region outside a circle centered at the origin with radius \(\sqrt{2}\). The area of the entire circle is:
   \[
   \text{Area of the circle} = \pi (\sqrt{2})^2 = 2\pi \text{ square units}
   \]

3. **Determine the overlapping area:**

   The circle intersects the diamond at points \((-1, 1)\), \((1, -1)\), and \((-1, -1)\). The overlapping region is a semicircle with radius \(\sqrt{2}\). The area of this semicircle is:
   \[
   \text{Area of the semicircle} = \frac{1}{2} \times \pi (\sqrt{2})^2 = \pi \text{ square units}
   \]

4. **Calculate the desired area:**

   The area of the figure defined by the given inequalities is the area of the diamond minus the area of the overlapping semicircle:
   \[
   \text{Desired area} = 8 - \pi
   \]

Thus, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{8 - \pi}

```<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a RESTful API using Flask for a web application that supports basic CRUD operations on a collection of items. Your API must include the following endpoints:

- `GET /items`: Retrieve a list of all items.
- `GET /items/<id>`: Retrieve a specific item by its ID. If the item does not exist, return a 404 error.
- `POST /items`: Create a new item. The request body should be a JSON object containing the item data. If the request body is invalid, return a 400 error.
- `PUT /items/<id>`: Update an existing item. The request body should be a JSON object containing the updated item data. If the item does not exist or the request body is invalid, return a 404 or 400 error, respectively.
- `DELETE /items/<id>`: Delete an item by its ID. If the item does not exist, return a 404 error.

**Sample Input/Output:**

1. **GET /items**
   - Response: `{"1": {"name": "Item 1", "description": "This is item 1"}, "2": {"name": "Item 2", "description": "This is item 2"}}`

2. **GET /items/1**
   - Response: `{"name": "Item 1", "description": "This is item 1"}`

3. **POST /items**
   - Request Body: `{"name": "Item 3", "description": "This is item 3"}`
   - Response: `{"name": "Item 3", "description": "This is item 3"}`

4. **PUT /items/1**
   - Request Body: `{"name": "Updated Item 1", "description": "Updated description"}`
   - Response: `{"name": "Updated Item 1", "description": "Updated description"}`

5. **DELETE /items/2**
   - Response: `{"result": true}`

```python
from flask import Flask, jsonify, request, abort

app = Flask(__name__)

# Sample data as a dictionary to mimic a database
items = {
    1: {'name': 'Item 1', 'description': 'This is item 1'},
    2: {'name': 'Item 2', 'description': 'This is item 2'}
}

def find_item(item_id):
    """
    Helper function to find an item by its ID.
    
    :param item_id: ID of the item to find.
    :return: Item dictionary if found, otherwise None.
    """
    item_id = int(item_id)
    return items.get(item_id)

@app.route('/items', methods=['GET'])
def get_items():
    """
    Retrieve a list of all items.
    
    :return: JSON response containing all items.
    """
    return jsonify(items)

@app.route('/items/<id>', methods=['GET'])
def get_item(id):
    """
    Retrieve a specific item by its ID.
    
    :param id: ID of the item to retrieve.
    :return: JSON response containing the item if found, otherwise 404 error.
    """
    item = find_item(id)
    if item is None:
        abort(404)
    return jsonify(item)

@app.route('/items', methods=['POST'])
def create_item():
    """
    Create a new item.
    
    :return: JSON response containing the created item and 201 status code if successful, otherwise 400 error.
    """
    if not request.json or 'name' not in request.json:
        abort(400)
    item_id = max(items.keys()) + 1 if items else 1
    item = {
        'name': request.json['name'],
        'description': request.json.get('description', "")
    }
    items[item_id] = item
    return jsonify(item), 201

@app.route('/items/<id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_item(id):
    """
    Update an existing item.
    
    :param id: ID of the item to update.
    :return: JSON response containing the updated item if successful, otherwise 404 or 400 error.
    """
    item = find_item(id)
    if item is None:
        abort(404)
    if not request.json:
        abort(400)
    item['name'] = request.json.get('name', item['name'])
    item['description'] = request.json.get('description', item['description'])
    return jsonify(item)

@app.route('/items/<id>', methods=['DELETE'])
def delete_item(id):
    """
    Delete an item by its ID.
    
    :param id: ID of the item to delete.
    :return: JSON response indicating successful deletion if item exists, otherwise 404 error.
    """
    item = find_item(id)
    if item is None:
        abort(404)
    del items[int(id)]
    return jsonify({'result': True})

@app.errorhandler(404)
def not_found(error):
    """
    Handle 404 Not Found errors.
    
    :param error: Error object.
    :return: JSON response with error message and 404 status code.
    """
    return jsonify({'error': 'Not found'}), 404

@app.errorhandler(400)
def bad_request(error):
    """
    Handle 400 Bad Request errors.
    
    :param error: Error object.
    :return: JSON response with error message and 400 status code.
    """
    return jsonify({'error': 'Bad request'}), 400

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)

1//1 (0); then start of string.

Then when string closes via the triplet, the next part is ;print(6). So the entire code would be:

0; some string; ;print(6) ?

Which would print 6 in Python.

But the first number was to be the Python's output. So maybe I got the order reversed here?

Ah, wait the problem says that when run in language A, outputs the first number, language B outputs the second. So if the languages are JS and Python, the first number is for JS, the second is for Python.

Wait in the example:

The generator receives (x,y), where x is the JS output (first) and y the Python output (second). 

So using that example, the Python code's output should be y (6). Which matches what we have here.

So in the generated code, the Python will see the ; ; (from first line semicolon and then after closing string) then print(y). 

Wait so the first number (x=5) is printed in JS via console.log(x), the second (y=6) is printed in Python via print(y).

Thus the example works as intended.

```   <#sep#>You are given a JSON file containing nested data structures. Your task is to write a Python script that reads this JSON file, normalizes the data, and writes it to a new JSON file. The normalization process involves the following steps:
- Convert all string values to lowercase.
- Replace all underscores with spaces in string values.
- Strip leading and trailing whitespace from string values.

**Input:**
- A JSON file named `input.json` with the following content:
```json
{
    "First_Name": "JOHN",
    "Last_Name": "DOE",
    "Address": {
        "Street_Name": "123_MAIN_STREET",
        "City": "NEW_YORK",
        "ZIP_Code": "10001"
    },
    "Phone_Numbers": ["123-456-7890", "987-654-3210"]
}
```

**Output:**
- A new JSON file named `normalized_output.json` with the following content:
```json
{
    "first name": "john",
    "last name": "doe",
    "address": {
        "street name": "123 main street",
        "city": "new york",
        "zip code": "10001"
    },
    "phone numbers": [
        "123-456-7890",
        "987-654-3210"
    ]
}
```

```python
import json

def normalize_value(value):
    """
    Normalize a single value by converting it to lowercase, replacing underscores with spaces,
    and stripping leading and trailing whitespace if it is a string.
    """
    if isinstance(value, str):
        return value.lower().replace('_', ' ').strip()
    return value

def normalize_dict(d):
    """
    Recursively normalize a dictionary by applying normalization to all string values,
    including keys and values in nested dictionaries and lists.
    """
    normalized_dict = {}
    for key, value in d.items():
        if isinstance(value, dict):
            normalized_dict[normalize_value(key)] = normalize_dict(value)
        elif isinstance(value, list):
            normalized_dict[normalize_value(key)] = [normalize_value(item) for item in value]
        else:
            normalized_dict[normalize_value(key)] = normalize_value(value)
    return normalized_dict

def read_json_file(filename):
    """
    Read and parse a JSON file, returning the data as a Python dictionary.
    """
    with open(filename, 'r') as file:
        return json.load(file)

def write_json_file(data, filename):
    """
    Write a Python dictionary to a JSON file with pretty printing (indentation).
    """
    with open(filename, 'w') as file:
        json.dump(data, file, indent=4)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    input_file = 'input.json'
    output_file = 'normalized_output.json'
    
    data = read_json_file(input_file)
    normalized_data = normalize_dict(data)
    write_json_file(normalized_data, output_file)

<#sep#>Make green work with nose_parameterized
Green doesn't work with `nose_parameterized` since it executes tests that `nose_parameterized` [marks](https://github.com/wolever/nose-parameterized/blob/master/nose_parameterized/parameterized.py#L232) as disabled using the nose-specific [`__test__`](https://github.com/nose-devs/nose/blob/master/nose/tools/nontrivial.py#L140) attribute

This attribute is easy to detect, so we should prune any tests that have it set.

diff --git a/green/loader.py b/green/loader.py

--- a/green/loader.py
+++ b/green/loader.py
@@ -121,11 +121,21 @@ def findDottedModuleAndParentDir(file_path):
     return (dotted_module, parent_dir)
 
 
+def isNoseDisabledCase(test_case_class, attrname):
+    test_func = getattr(test_case_class, attrname)
+    nose_enabled = getattr(test_func, "__test__", None)
+
+    if nose_enabled is False:
+        return True
+    else:
+        return False
+
 def loadFromTestCase(test_case_class):
     debug("Examining test case {}".format(test_case_class.__name__), 3)
     test_case_names = list(filter(
         lambda attrname: (attrname.startswith('test') and
-                          callable(getattr(test_case_class, attrname))),
+                          callable(getattr(test_case_class, attrname)) and
+                          not isNoseDisabledCase(test_case_class, attrname)),
         dir(test_case_class)))
     debug("Test case names: {}".format(test_case_names))

```   866     and y1: "y > <#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple banking system. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Create Account**: Given an account holder's name and an initial balance, create a new bank account with a unique account number.
2. **Deposit**: Given an amount, deposit it into the specified account.
3. **Withdraw**: Given an amount, withdraw it from the specified account if sufficient funds are available.
4. **Display**: Display the account holder's name, account number, and current balance.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 100), the number of operations.
- The next `n` lines describe the operations. Each operation is one of the following:
  - `CREATE <name> <initial_balance>`: Create a new account with the given name and initial balance.
  - `DEPOSIT <account_number> <amount>`: Deposit the specified amount into the account with the given account number.
  - `WITHDRAW <account_number> <amount>`: Withdraw the specified amount from the account with the given account number.
  - `DISPLAY <account_number>`: Display the details of the account with the given account number.

**Output:**
- For each `DEPOSIT`, `WITHDRAW`, and `DISPLAY` operation, output the appropriate message as specified in the solution.

**Sample Input:**
```
5
CREATE JohnDoe 1000
DEPOSIT 1000 500
WITHDRAW 1000 200
DISPLAY 1000
WITHDRAW 1000 1500
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Deposited 500. New balance is 1500.
Withdrew 200. New balance is 1300.
Account Holder: JohnDoe
Account Number: 1000
Current Balance: 1300
Insufficient funds.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    account_number_counter = 1000  # Unique account number generator starting from 1000

    def __init__(self, account_holder_name, initial_balance):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account with a unique account number, account holder's name, and initial balance.
        """
        self.account_number = BankAccount.account_number_counter
        BankAccount.account_number_counter += 1  # Increment the account number counter
        self.account_holder_name = account_holder_name
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account if the amount is positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Deposited {amount}. New balance is {self.balance}.")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew {amount}. New balance is {self.balance}.")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def display(self):
        """
        Display the account holder's name, account number, and current balance.
        """
        print(f"Account Holder: {self.account_holder_name}")
        print(f"Account Number: {self.account_number}")
        print(f"Current Balance: {self.balance}")


# Example usage:
# Create an account
my_account = BankAccount("John Doe", 1000)

# Display account details
my_account.display()

# Deposit money
my_account.deposit(500)

# Withdraw money
my_account.withdraw(200)

# Attempt to withdraw more money than the balance
my_account.withdraw(1500)

# Display account details again
my_account.display()

something along these lines... (some code courtesy of [geopy](https://github.com/geopy/geopy/blob/master/geopy/distance.py)):

```python

#             model             major (km)   minor (km)     flattening
ELLIPSOIDS = {'WGS-84':        (6378.137,    6356.7523142,  1 / 298.257223563),
              'GRS-80':        (6378.137,    6356.7523141,  1 / 298.257222101),
              'Airy (1830)':   (6377.563396, 6356.256909,   1 / 299.3249646),
              'Intl 1924':     (6378.388,    6356.911946,   1 / 297.0),
              'Clarke (1880)': (6378.249145, 6356.51486955, 1 / 293.465),
              'GRS-67':        (6378.1600,   6356.774719,   1 / 298.25),
              }


EARTH_RADIUS = 6371.009

def great_circle(ax, ay, bx, by, radius=EARTH_RADIUS):
    '''
    calculate the great circle distance (km) between points
    
    Provide points (ax, ay) and (bx, by) as floats, or as 
    vectors. If ax and ay are vectors or arrays of the
    same shape, the element-wise distance will be found
    between points in the vectors/arrays. If ax, ay are
    (Mx1) column vectors and (bx, by) are (1xN) row 
    vectors, the vectors will be broadcast using numpy 
    broadcasting rules and the distance between each pair
    of points will be returned as an (MxN) matrix.

    Parameters
    -----------
    ax : float or array
        x/long of point a
    ay : float or array
        y/lat of point a
    bx : float or array
        x/long of point b
    by : float or array
        y/lat of point b
    radius : float, optional
        Radius of the sphere on which to calculate the great
        circle distance (default is to use the Earth's radius in
        km, `6371.009`). Values returned will be in units of the
        radius provided. 

    Returns
    --------
    distance : float or array
        great circle distance between points a and b. Units will
        match the radius provided (default km)
    '''

    lat1, lng1 = np.radians(ay), np.radians(ax)
    lat2, lng2 = np.radians(by), np.radians(bx)

    sin_lat1, cos_lat1 = np.sin(lat1), np.cos(lat1)
    sin_lat2, cos_lat2 = np.sin(lat2), np.cos(lat2)

    delta_lng = lng2 - lng1
    cos_delta_lng, sin_delta_lng = np.cos(delta_lng), np.sin(delta_lng)

    d = np.arctan2(np.sqrt((cos_lat2 * sin_delta_lng) ** 2 +
                   (cos_lat1 * sin_lat2 -
                    sin_lat1 * cos_lat2 * cos_delta_lng) ** 2),
              sin_lat1 * sin_lat2 + cos_lat1 * cos_lat2 * cos_delta_lng)

    return radius * d
```

```   883<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Car` class in Python. The class should have the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `make`: A string representing the manufacturer of the car.
  - `model`: A string representing the model of the car.
  - `year`: An integer representing the year the car was manufactured.
  - `mileage`: An integer representing the current mileage of the car.
  - `color`: A string representing the color of the car.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, make, model, year, mileage, color)`: Initializes the car with the given attributes.
  - `paint(self, new_color)`: Changes the color of the car to `new_color`.
  - `drive(self, distance)`: Increases the mileage by `distance` if `distance` is positive.
  - `service(self)`: Resets the mileage to 0.
  - `display_info(self)`: Prints the car's details in the format: `"Car Info: {year} {make} {model}, Color: {color}, Mileage: {mileage} miles"`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
Input:
car = Car("Toyota", "Camry", 2020, 15000, "Blue")
car.display_info()
car.paint("Red")
car.drive(250)
car.display_info()
car.service()
car.display_info()

Output:
Car Info: 2020 Toyota Camry, Color: Blue, Mileage: 15000 miles
Car Info: 2020 Toyota Camry, Color: Red, Mileage: 15250 miles
Car Info: 2020 Toyota Camry, Color: Red, Mileage: 0 miles
```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, mileage, color):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance with the specified attributes.

        :param make: The manufacturer of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year the car was manufactured.
        :param mileage: The current mileage of the car.
        :param color: The color of the car.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        self.mileage = mileage
        self.color = color

    def paint(self, new_color):
        """
        Changes the color of the car.

        :param new_color: The new color to paint the car.
        """
        self.color = new_color

    def drive(self, distance):
        """
        Increases the mileage of the car by the specified distance if the distance is positive.

        :param distance: The distance driven.
        """
        if distance > 0:
            self.mileage += distance

    def service(self):
        """
        Resets the mileage of the car to 0.
        """
        self.mileage = 0

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the car's details in a formatted string.
        """
        print(f"Car Info: {self.year} {self.make} {self.model}, Color: {self.color}, Mileage: {self.mileage} miles")

+def _reindex_spatial_data_to_regions(ds, df):
+    '''
+    Reindexes spatial and segment weight data to regions
+    Enables region index-based math operations
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    ds: xarray Dataset
+    df: pandas DataFrame
+    Returns
+    -------
+    Xarray DataArray
+    '''
+
+    # use vectorized indexing in xarray >= 0.10
+    if LooseVersion(xr.__version__) > LooseVersion('0.9.999'):
+
+        lon_indexer = xr.DataArray(df.lon.values, dims=('reshape_index', ))
+        lat_indexer = xr.DataArray(df.lat.values, dims=('reshape_index', ))
+
+        return ds.sel(lon=lon_indexer, lat=lat_indexer)
+
+    else:
+        res = ds.sel_points(
+            'reshape_index',
+            lat=df.lat.values,
+            lon=df.lon.values)
+
+        return res
+
+
+def _aggregate_reindexed_data_to_regions(
+        ds,
+        variable,
+        aggwt,
+        agglev,
+        weights,
+        backup_aggwt='areawt'):
+    '''
+    Performs weighted avg for climate variable by region
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+
+    ds: xarray.DataArray
+
+    variable: str
+        name of the data variable
+
+    aggwt: str
+        variable to weight by (i.e popwt, areawt, cropwt)
+
+    agglev: str
+        indicates which regional id scheme to select in the dataframe
+
+    weight: pd.DataFrame
+        pandas DataFrame of weights
+
+    backup_aggwt: str, optional
+        aggregation weight to use in regions with no aggwt data (default
+        'areawt')
+
+    '''
+
+    ds.coords[agglev] = xr.DataArray(
+                weights[agglev].values,
+                dims={'reshape_index': weights.index.values})
+
+    # format weights
+    ds[aggwt] = xr.DataArray(
+                weights[aggwt].values,
+                dims={'reshape_index': weights.index.values})
+
+    ds[aggwt] = (
+        ds[aggwt]
+        .where(ds[aggwt] > 0)
+        .fillna(weights[backup_aggwt].values))
+
+    weighted = xr.Dataset({
+        variable: (
+            (
+                (ds[variable]*ds[aggwt])
+                .groupby(agglev)
+                .sum(dim='reshape_index')) /
+            (
+                ds[aggwt]
+                .groupby(agglev)
+                .sum(dim='reshape_index')))})
+
+    return weighted
+
+
+'''
+================
+Public Functions
+================
+'''
+

+    Parameters

+        File path or dataset

+        Variable name to be read
+

+        'longitude'])
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    xr.Dataset
+         xarray dataset loaded into memory

+
+    if lon_name is not None:
+        lon_names = [lon_name]
+
+    if hasattr(fp, 'sel_points'):
+        ds = fp
+
+    else:
+        with xr.open_dataset(fp) as ds:
+            ds.load()
+
+    _fill_holes_xr(ds, varname, broadcast_dims=broadcast_dims)
+    return _standardize_longitude_dimension(ds, lon_names=lon_names)

+        'longitude'])
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    xr.Dataset
+         xarray dataset loaded into memory
+    '''
+
+    if lon_name is not None:
+        lon_names = [lon_name]
+
+    if broadcast_dims is None:
+        broadcast_dims = tuple([])
+
+    if hasattr(fp, 'sel_points'):
+        ds = fp
+
+    else:
+        with xr.open_dataset(fp) as ds:
+            ds.load()
+
+    if 'lat' in ds.data_vars:
+        ds = ds.set_coords('lat')
+        ds = ds.swap_dims({'nlat': 'lat'})
+
+    if 'lon' in ds.data_vars:
+        ds = ds.set_coords('lon')
+        ds = ds.swap_dims({'nlon': 'lon'})
+
+    _fill_holes_xr(ds, varname, broadcast_dims=broadcast_dims)
+    return _standardize_longitude_dimension(ds, lon_names=lon_names)

1. **Add a Book**: Add a new book to the inventory with its title, author, and stock count. If the book already exists, raise a `ValueError`.
2. **Remove a Book**: Remove a book from the inventory by its title. If the book does not exist, raise a `KeyError`.
3. **Search for a Book**: Search for a book by either its title or author. If searching by title and the book does not exist, return an empty dictionary. If searching by author, return all books by that author.
4. **Update Stock**: Update the stock count of a book by its title. If the book does not exist, raise a `KeyError`.
5. **List Books**: Return a dictionary of all books in the inventory.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: `add_book("The Great Gatsby", "F. Scott Fitzgerald", 12)`
- Input: `add_book("1984", "George Orwell", 8)`
- Input: `search_book(title="1984")`
- Output: `{'1984': ('George Orwell', 8)}`
- Input: `search_book(author="F. Scott Fitzgerald")`
- Output: `{'The Great Gatsby': ('F. Scott Fitzgerald', 12)}`
- Input: `update_stock("1984", 10)`
- Input: `list_books()`
- Output: `{'The Great Gatsby': ('F. Scott Fitzgerald', 12), '1984': ('George Orwell', 10)}`
- Input: `remove_book("The Great Gatsby")`
- Input: `list_books()`
- Output: `{'1984': ('George Orwell', 10)}`

```python
class BookstoreInventory:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize the inventory dictionary."""
        self.inventory = {}

    def add_book(self, title, author, stock_count):
        """Add a new book to the inventory. Raise ValueError if the book already exists."""
        if title in self.inventory:
            raise ValueError("Book already exists in inventory.")
        self.inventory[title] = (author, stock_count)

    def remove_book(self, title):
        """Remove a book from the inventory. Raise KeyError if the book does not exist."""
        if title not in self.inventory:
            raise KeyError("Book not found in inventory.")
        del self.inventory[title]

    def search_book(self, title=None, author=None):
        """Search for a book by title or author. Return an empty dictionary if searching by title and the book does not exist.
        Return all books by the author if searching by author. Raise ValueError if neither title nor author is provided."""
        if title:
            return {title: self.inventory[title]} if title in self.inventory else {}
        elif author:
            return {k: v for k, v in self.inventory.items() if v[0] == author}
        else:
            raise ValueError("Either title or author must be provided for search.")

    def update_stock(self, title, stock_count):
        """Update the stock count of a book. Raise KeyError if the book does not exist."""
        if title not in self.inventory:
            raise KeyError("Book not found in inventory.")
        author, _ = self.inventory[title]
        self.inventory[title] = (author, stock_count)

    def list_books(self):
        """Return a dictionary of all books in the inventory."""
        return self.inventory
```<#sep#> k is an odd natural number. Since the problem might expect the form like \boxed{5k} where k is odd, but instructions say "put the answer (and only answer) inside \boxed{}". Hmm, perhaps the problem expects the answer as n ≡5 mod10, but in boxed form? Or list the form.

+#             model             major (km)   minor (km)     flattening
+ELLIPSOIDS = {'WGS-84':        (6378.137,    6356.7523142,  1 / 298.257223563),
+              'GRS-80':        (6378.137,    6356.7523141,  1 / 298.257222101),
+              'Airy (1830)':   (6377.563396, 6356.256909,   1 / 299.3249646),
+              'Intl 1924':     (6378.388,    6356.911946,   1 / 297.0),
+              'Clarke (1880)': (6378.249145, 6356.51486955, 1 / 293.465),
+              'GRS-67':        (6378.1600,   6356.774719,   1 / 298.25),
+              }
+
+
+EARTH_RADIUS = 6371.009
+
+def great_circle(ax, ay, bx, by, radius=EARTH_RADIUS):
+    '''
+    calculate the great circle distance (km) between points
+
+    Provide points (ax, ay) and (bx, by) as floats, or as
+    vectors. If ax and ay are vectors or arrays of the
+    same shape, the element-wise distance will be found
+    between points in the vectors/arrays. If ax, ay are
+    (Mx1) column vectors and (bx, by) are (1xN) row
+    vectors, the vectors will be broadcast using numpy
+    broadcasting rules and the distance between each pair
+    of points will be returned as an (MxN) matrix.
+
+    Parameters
+    -----------
+    ax : float or array
+        x/long of point a
+    ay : float or array
+        y/lat of point a
+    bx : float or array
+        x/long of point b
+    by : float or array
+        y/lat of point b
+    radius : float, optional
+        Radius of the sphere on which to calculate the great
+        circle distance (default is to use the Earth's radius in
+        km, `6371.009`). Values returned will be in units of the
+        radius provided.
+
+    Returns
+    --------
+    distance : float or array
+        great circle distance between points a and b. Units will
+        match the radius provided (default km)
+    '''
+
+    lat1, lng1 = np.radians(ay), np.radians(ax)
+    lat2, lng2 = np.radians(by), np.radians(bx)
+
+    sin_lat1, cos_lat1 = np.sin(lat1), np.cos(lat1)
+    sin_lat2, cos_lat2 = np.sin(lat2), np.cos(lat2)
+
+    delta_lng = lng2 - lng1
+    cos_delta_lng, sin_delta_lng = np.cos(delta_lng), np.sin(delta_lng)
+
+    d = np.arctan2(np.sqrt((cos_lat2 * sin_delta_lng) ** 2 +
+                   (cos_lat1 * sin_lat2 -
+                    sin_lat1 * cos_lat2 * cos_delta_lng) ** 2),
+              sin_lat1 * sin_lat2 + cos_lat1 * cos_lat2 * cos_delta_lng)
+
+    return radius * d

### What is a Cap Rate and How Does it Affect Real Estate Investments<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a vending machine simulation in Python. The vending machine should support the following operations:

1. **Initialization**: The machine should start with a predefined set of products and their prices.
2. **Accept Payment**: Given a list of coin values (in cents), calculate the total amount of money inserted.
3. **Dispense Product**: Given a product name and the amount paid, check if the product is available and if the payment is sufficient. If so, dispense the product and return the change. Otherwise, return an appropriate message.
4. **Show Products**: Display the list of available products along with their prices.

**Input**:
- The `accept_payment` method will receive a list of integers representing coin values in cents.
- The `dispense_product` method will receive a string (product name) and a float (amount paid).

**Output**:
- The `accept_payment` method should return a float representing the total amount of money inserted.
- The `dispense_product` method should return a string indicating the result of the transaction (dispensed product with change or an error message).
- The `show_products` method should print the available products and their prices.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: `coins_inserted = [25, 25, 25, 10, 10]`
- Input: `product = 'cola'`
- Output: `Dispensing cola. Change: $0.50`

```python
class VendingMachine:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initializes the vending machine with a set of products and their prices.
        """
        self.products = {
            'cola': 1.50,
            'chips': 1.25,
            'candy': 0.75
        }

    def accept_payment(self, coins):
        """
        Calculates the total amount of money inserted based on the coin values.
        
        :param coins: List of integers representing coin values in cents.
        :return: Total amount of money inserted as a float.
        """
        total = sum(coins) / 100  # Assuming the coins are in cents
        return total

    def dispense_product(self, product, amount_paid):
        """
        Dispenses the product if available and sufficient funds are provided.
        
        :param product: String representing the product name.
        :param amount_paid: Float representing the amount of money paid.
        :return: String indicating the result of the transaction.
        """
        if product not in self.products:
            return "Product not available."
        elif amount_paid < self.products[product]:
            return "Insufficient funds."
        else:
            change = amount_paid - self.products[product]
            return f"Dispensing {product}. Change: ${change:.2f}"

    def show_products(self):
        """
        Prints the available products and their prices.
        """
        print("Available products:")
        for product, price in self.products.items():
            print(f"{product}: ${price:.2f}")

# Example usage
vm = VendingMachine()
vm.show_products()

# Simulate inserting coins and buying a product
coins_inserted = [25, 25, 25, 10, 10]  # 1.00 + 0.50 + 0.50 = 2.00
amount_paid = vm.accept_payment(coins_inserted)
result = vm.dispense_product('cola', amount_paid)
print(result)
